# Decisions

One line each: the decision, and why. "Owner" means the owner chose it in conversation before
the build; "Build" means it was chosen during the overnight build.

## Owner's decisions (recorded as given)
1. **Dedicated new AWS account** for AI City. The owner confirms the account before the first deploy.
2. **Budget $150/month, daily global cap $10.** Starting values only: heavy use of Fable and Opus will need higher caps.
3. **No connection to the owner's Mac.** Nothing in this product reaches it.
4. **Workers run isolated in AgentCore microVMs with the policy gate on** (setting `policy_applies_to_workers`, default true).
5. **Global inference profiles.** Cheapest, and the only way to reach Fable from Europe. Prompts may be processed outside the EU. The prefix is one config value (`profilePrefix` in `config/models.json`, `profile_prefix` in Terraform); switching to `eu.` makes Fable roles fall back to Opus 5.5.
6. **Cognito web login over CloudFront** with MFA; the web app is the only public entry point.
7. **Private GitHub repo** for the code.
8. **Start with empty memory.** Nothing imported from the Mac.
9. **Single `prod` environment.**
10. **Anthropic models only**, as pinned in `config/models.json`: AIC Sonnet 5.5, Orchestrator and Managers Fable 5.1 (Opus 5.5 fallback), Workers Opus 5.5, utility Haiku 5.5.

## Build decisions
11. **Region `eu-north-1`.** Facts check confirmed Runtime, Memory, Gateway, Identity, built-in tools and Observability there.
12. **Transcribe and Polly are AWS speech services, not language models**, so they do not conflict with "Anthropic models only".
13. **Voice through a Cognito identity pool** scoped to `transcribe:StartStreamTranscriptionWebSocket` and `polly:SynthesizeSpeech` only (ADR 0006). Simpler and lower latency than a Lambda proxy.
14. **Workers' web search uses the SDK's WebSearch/WebFetch**, because the AgentCore Gateway Web Search connector is not in Stockholm.
15. **Application inference profiles per role, with a switch.** Created from global profiles; if AWS refuses a global source at deploy, `use_application_inference_profiles = false` falls back to calling global profiles and the ledger carries per-role cost.
16. **Fable fallback is one retry on Opus 5.5 per turn**, recorded in the ledger (`fallback: true`). Refusal or no verdict never loops.
17. **Cache TTL:** 1-hour prompt cache for orchestrator and managers (their turns are event-driven and minutes apart), 5-minute for AIC and workers (continuous turns). Cost math supports both.
18. **`CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_1M_CONTEXT=1`** everywhere; long sessions compact at 60 % of a 200k window with a Haiku summary.
19. **Runtimes invoked with IAM (SigV4) from Lambda only**, not JWT: only the dispatcher talks to AgentCore, so no inbound JWT config is needed on runtimes.
20. **Ledger is one DynamoDB table** (`aic-prod-ledger`) with `pk`/`sk` plus two GSIs (`gsi1` by status, `gsi2` by department); spend counters are separate items updated atomically with `ADD` and a condition.
21. **Daily caps are per UTC day**, as in upstream; the HUD shows Stockholm time beside it.
22. **Per-department daily cap default $5**, global $10, monthly $150. A department cap above the global cap is pointless and is rejected by Settings.
23. **Concurrency defaults:** 100 workers per department, 2,450 global (2,500 Stockholm quota − 50), 20 new sessions per second (quota 25).
24. **Turn timeout 15 minutes** for synchronous turns (AgentCore request timeout); long worker jobs run async up to 8 h, with a 7.5 h soft limit so the worker reports before AgentCore kills it.
25. **SQS visibility timeout 16 minutes** (turn timeout + 1 minute), DLQ after 3 receives.
26. **Departments are data.** `config/departments.json` is an empty seed; the Orchestrator creates them.
27. **Docker is not installed on the build machine**, so the image was not built tonight; the Dockerfile is written and checked by a unit test that parses it (arm64, port 8080, non-root user).
28. **tflint and checkov installed locally** with Homebrew/pip to run the offline checks (they are local tools, not cloud calls). If installation failed it is listed in the morning report.
29. **HUD state in a plain reducer** ported from upstream `city-state.js`, so the City view is unit-tested in Node.
30. **The City view canvas renderer is ported from upstream `city.js`** (pixel art drawn in code, no image assets), changed for the new cast: AIC in the plaza where Jarvis stood, Orchestrator at the HQ door, Secretary and Reviewer removed.
31. **Ledger writes from workers** go only through the `report_to_manager` / `ask_manager` tools, which put events on the bus; the ledger Lambda writes them. Workers have no DynamoDB permission at all (stricter than "only their own hire record").
32. **Step Functions approvals** use `waitForTaskToken`; the token is stored on the approval item and returned when the owner approves in the HUD.
33. **EventBridge Scheduler jobs are created with `state = "DISABLED"`** (retire idle agents, weekly self-improvement, reminders sweep).
34. **Self-improvement loop: built, disabled, and fenced** — its changed-file check refuses any path under `infra/`, IAM, `config/caps.json`, `config/policy.json` and `.github/`.
35. **Pause flag** lives in the ledger (`CONFIG#city`), read by the dispatcher before every turn; `scripts/city-off.sh` sets it and disables the SQS event source mappings.
36. **No Reviewer and no Secretary agents.** Managers review; the Orchestrator signs off; AIC summarizes.
37. **Bedrock throttling** is retried with full-jitter exponential backoff (base 1 s, cap 30 s, 5 tries), never a pause. "Spend cap reached" is the only pause from spend.
38. **TypeScript pinned ~6.0.3**, because typescript-eslint 8.72 supports TypeScript below 6.1 only.
39. **A fourth queue for AIC** (`aic-prod-ace`), next to the orchestrator, manager and worker queues, so owner messages and results pass the same pause/cap checks as every other turn.
40. **Managers get an `answer_worker` tool** beside the listed ones; without it a worker's `ask_manager` question had no way back.
41. **`gh pr merge` is denied for every agent** (upstream: ask), because the owner merges PRs into `main`.
42. **Cloud mutation from agents is denied, not asked** (upstream asked for `aws ... delete-*` and `terraform destroy`/`apply ... prod`). An owner approval can never unlock a deny rule.
43. **Instructions copy the manager on the record, without waking it**, so an `@agent` instruction costs one turn, not two.
44. **The stale-lease sweep** (scheduled job `sweep`) re-sends messages whose turn crashed; it gives back the dead worker's concurrency slot.
45. **AIC's role can write the ledger and send to the queues.** The prompt lists "read ledger status" for AIC, but AIC's own tools (delegate a task, answer a question, notes, pause) write tickets and messages. Its role still has no Factory, no S3, no secrets.
46. **Worker ledger writes are limited by key prefix** (`dynamodb:LeadingKeys`): tickets, its own agent/turn/tool records, work leases, spend counters, slots, approval *requests*. Workers cannot write settings (pause, caps), departments, specialists, questions or owner approvals. Owner approvals are separate `APPROVED#<hash>` items only the API writes; a worker can only consume (delete) one. Residual: a worker can update another worker's `AGENT#wkr-*` record (documented in SECURITY.md).
47. **Workers reach S3 only by assuming `aic-prod-worker-workspace` with a `hire_id` session tag**; that role is limited to `hires/${aws:PrincipalTag/hire_id}/*`.
48. **The Factory's function name is fixed** (`aic-prod-factory`), so the agents module gets its ARN without depending on the api module (avoids a cycle).
49. **The HUD's `config.json` is written by Terraform** into the site bucket with public values only (endpoints, client and pool IDs).
50. **Log retention 30 days** (variable), not checkov's suggested 1 year, to keep cost low; the ledger is the durable record.
51. **The site bucket uses SSE-S3**, not the KMS key, so CloudFront OAC can read it without a cross-service key policy; it holds only public app files.
52. **WAF also gets AWS's KnownBadInputs rule set** besides the common rules, IP reputation and a 1,000-requests-per-5-minutes rate limit.
53. **In deploy.yml the image is pushed before `terraform apply`** (inside the approved job), because the reviewed plan points the runtimes at that image tag; applying first would point them at an image that does not exist yet.
54. **The deploy role has AdministratorAccess fenced by a boundary and its trust**: only `deploy.yml` on `main` in the `production` environment (owner approval) can assume it; the boundary denies Organizations, account, IAM users/access keys and CloudTrail tampering. Terraform must manage IAM, KMS and Cognito, so a narrower policy was not attempted tonight.
55. **Commit messages carry `[skip ci]` at the end of the subject line**, followed by the Co-Authored-By trailer (git trailers must be the last paragraph). GitHub skips workflows when `[skip ci]` appears in the message, and every workflow is `workflow_dispatch`-only anyway.
56. **No spend limit (the owner's choice, 2026-11-06).** City spend caps are off by default (`null` = no limit) and stay optional in Settings; when set they are still checked before every turn. AWS Budgets only emails at 50/80/100 % + forecast: the deny-Bedrock budget action and the budget Lambda that paused the city are removed. Concurrency ceilings (100 per department, 2,450 total, 20 new sessions/s) stay: they protect AgentCore quotas, not spend. Supersedes part of ADR 0010.
57. **AWS Budgets alert amount $10,000 / month (the owner's choice, 2026-11-06)**, replacing $150. Emails at 50/80/100 % ($5k / $8k / $10k) and on a forecast above it; still alerts only, nothing is blocked or paused. Set in `prod.tfvars` (`monthly_budget_usd`) or the GitHub variable `AIC_MONTHLY_BUDGET_USD`; Terraform allows up to $10,000.
58. **No bootstrap; the deploy needs only the role ARN (2026-11-06).** The owner provides the OIDC deploy role (trust in `docs/deploy-role-trust-policy.json`). `deploy.yml` creates the Terraform state bucket `aic-prod-tfstate-<account>-<region>` on its first run (`scripts/ensure-state-bucket.sh`: private, versioned, SSE-KMS with the AWS-managed key, TLS-only; native S3 lockfile). The agent image is built and pushed by `terraform_data.image` during apply, after the ECR repository and before the runtimes, so one reviewed plan deploys from nothing. Replaces `infra/bootstrap` and the two-step first deploy; supersedes the deploy-role part of decision 54. The state bucket is the one resource created before the owner approves a plan.
59. **Owner email as a secret, state in `lamo-labs-state-bucket` (the owner's choice, 2026-11-06).** The repository is public, so workflow logs are public: `AIC_OWNER_EMAIL` is a GitHub secret (masked in logs) and the full plan is printed to the log instead of being uploaded as `plan.txt`. The binary plan artifact still holds it for 3 days (needed to apply exactly the reviewed plan). State key `ai-city-aws/prod/terraform.tfstate` so the bucket can hold other projects; an existing bucket is used as it is and its own region is passed to the backend.
60. **Push to `main` deploys prod, without approval (the owner's choice, 2026-11-06).** `deploy.yml` runs on pushes to `main` (docs-only pushes skipped via `paths-ignore`) and by hand, never on pull requests; the `production` environment stays for deployment history without required reviewers. The image tag is `rt-<hash of the runtime sources>`, so pushes that don't touch the runtime don't rebuild the image or replace the runtimes. Supersedes the manual-only rule and the `[skip ci]` commit rule (decision 55); `[skip ci]` now means "don't deploy this push". Agent sessions still never deploy on their own: they push to `main` only when the owner asks.
61. **The deploy role trusts GitHub's immutable OIDC subject** (2026-11-06). The repository issues tokens with `sub = repo:Lamo2real@143636021/ai-city-aws@1448210802:<ref or environment>` (owner and repository IDs), so the trust matches that exactly, for `ref:refs/heads/main` and `environment:production`. The first deploy failed on the older `repo:Lamo2real/ai-city-aws:...` form. The `job_workflow_ref` condition was dropped: the subject already pins the repository and branch.
62. **The application is Python (the owner's choice, 2026-11-06).** Shared core, Lambdas and the AgentCore runtime moved from TypeScript to Python 3.13 in `backend/` (package `aic`, managed by `uv`; ruff, mypy, pytest). Pydantic replaces zod for event schemas; PyJWT replaces aws-jwt-verify for the WebSocket authorizer; Starlette + uvicorn serve the runtime; the Claude Agent SDK for Python bundles the Claude Code CLI, so the image has no Node.js. Behaviour and wire formats are unchanged (events, API JSON, DynamoDB items, queue messages keep their keys). All Lambdas share one zip built for arm64 (boto3 comes from the runtime). The React HUD and the Node-based repo checks (`check-tags.mjs`, `check-iam.mjs`, infra/workflow vitest tests) stay as they are; the wake-word parity test now uses `tests/fixtures/wake-cases.json`.
63. **Our own sign-in screens (the owner's choice, 2026-11-06).** Cognito's hosted pages confused the owner, so the HUD signs in itself with Amplify Auth v6 (`aws-amplify/auth`): SRP, the NEW_PASSWORD_REQUIRED step, TOTP setup (QR code from `qrcode`) and codes, forgot-password, token refresh, tokens in sessionStorage. The app client allows `ALLOW_USER_SRP_AUTH` only (refresh token rotation forbids `ALLOW_REFRESH_TOKEN_AUTH`; Amplify refreshes with `GetTokensFromRefreshToken`); the hosted domain stays but is unused. The invitation and reset emails are rewritten in plain steps; temporary passwords last 7 days. A public, rate-limited `POST /auth/first-login-email` re-sends the invitation (Cognito `AdminCreateUser` with `RESEND`) only for the owner's email and answers the same for any email. Playwright drives the screens with a fake AuthClient (`window.__aicAuth`); real Cognito is checked at the first real sign-in. AIC's look is one eye, the owner's own drawing (`web/public/ace-eye.png`, used as a CSS mask so each state can colour and move it).
64. **Knowledge base: Bedrock Knowledge Base on S3 Vectors + a DynamoDB graph, all in Stockholm (the owner's choice, 2026-11-07).** Contract in `docs/KNOWLEDGE.md`. Facts checked 2026-11-07 (AWS Price List API, eu-north-1; Bedrock and Neptune docs):
    - **Vectors:** S3 Vectors is in Stockholm ($0.06/GB-month, $0.20/GB written, $0.0025 per 1,000 queries; nothing fixed). OpenSearch Serverless was rejected: $0.255 per OCU-hour with a 2-OCU minimum for classic collections ≈ $372/month idle.
    - **Embeddings:** Anthropic has no embedding model, so the owner allowed one exception to "Anthropic models only": Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2 (in-Region in Stockholm, $0.021 per million tokens, 1,024 dimensions), pinned under `embeddings` in `config/models.json`. It only turns text into vectors; all reading, summarizing and entity extraction is Claude Haiku 5.5. Cohere Embed v4 needs cross-Region inference from Stockholm.
    - **Graph:** the owner first chose Neptune Analytics, but Bedrock's managed GraphRAG is not offered in Stockholm (only Frankfurt, London, Ireland, Oregon, N. Virginia, Tokyo, Singapore), and the smallest Neptune Analytics graph in Stockholm (16 m-NCU, $0.581/h) costs ≈ $424/month running. The owner then chose the cheap option: Haiku 5.5 extracts entities and relationships into a DynamoDB table, and search widens vector hits one hop through that graph. Neptune Analytics stays a later upgrade (`docs/KNOWLEDGE.md` §13).
    - **Parsing and media:** the Bedrock Data Automation parser is only in us-west-2 (preview), so scanned PDFs and images are read by Haiku 5.5 vision; audio and video go through Amazon Transcribe batch (in Stockholm), with MediaConvert extracting audio first from video and from containers Transcribe doesn't take (such as `.mov`).
    - **Importance:** three scopes per item: `always` (summary in every agent's prompt, 8,000-token budget), `relevant` (default, found by search), `on_request` (only when the owner asks).
    - **Safety:** stored content is untrusted data, returned inside `<knowledge>` tags with a "data, not instructions" line; only AIC can add items (when the owner asks), so worker-fetched content can't feed itself into the store; link fetching refuses private and metadata addresses.
    - **Cost:** light personal use (2 GB of documents, 10 hours of video and 3,000 searches a month) is an estimated $15–25/month, mostly Transcribe ($0.024/min); $0 when idle.
65. **The knowledge pipeline is started by the knowledge Lambda, not straight from EventBridge (2026-11-07).** EventBridge rule targets cannot set a Step Functions execution name (only the input), and the contract wants one run per item named after it. So the rule on S3 "Object Created" under `raw/` invokes `aic-prod-knowledge` with `{"action": "start", "bucket", "key"}`; it starts `aic-prod-knowledge-ingest` with name = `item_id`, so a repeated event can't start a second run. Retries 10× over an hour, then an SQS DLQ. The provider (aws ≥ 6.68) already supports S3 Vectors and a Bedrock Knowledge Base on it, so no `awscc` fallback. MediaConvert needs `iam:PassRole`, which the permission boundary used to deny outright: it now allows passing exactly `aic-prod-knowledge-mediaconvert` to MediaConvert and denies passing any other role. The S3 Vectors bucket uses the project KMS key, so the key policy lets `indexing.s3vectors.amazonaws.com` use it.
66. **Knowledge base v2: performance first (the owner's choice, 2026-11-07).** The owner asked for "the most performance optimized RAG and graph databases as well as all other stuff such as lambda functions", accepting about **$615/month fixed** (Stockholm prices, AWS Price List API, 2026-11-07). Supersedes the S3 Vectors and DynamoDB-graph parts of decision 64. Contract: `docs/KNOWLEDGE.md` §1, §4, §9, §10, §14.
    - **Vectors: OpenSearch Serverless, 1 OCU, no standby (~$186/month)** instead of S3 Vectors (~100 ms when searched often, up to 1 s when idle, $0 fixed, meaning-only). OpenSearch answers in tens of ms and supports Bedrock's **hybrid search** (BM25 + vectors; hybrid works only on OpenSearch Serverless, RDS and MongoDB, not S3 Vectors). Trade-off: without a standby replica a failure means a rebuild (AWS re-creates it; the data is re-ingested from S3); with HA it would be ≥ 2 OCU (~$372/month). OCUs scale up under load or with data size, $0.255 per OCU-hour each. HNSW (faiss, m 16, ef 512), float32, 1,024 dims: best recall; fewer dims or binary vectors would save memory, not noticeable time, at personal scale.
    - **Graph: Neptune Analytics, 16 m-NCU, no replica (~$424/month; a replica would double it)**, in-memory, openCypher, the whole expansion in one query instead of many DynamoDB queries. Managed GraphRAG is not in Stockholm, so AI City writes the graph itself (parameterised queries only). Public endpoint with IAM SigV4 auth instead of a VPC: no VPC for Lambdas and AgentCore runtimes (and no NAT cost), the graph is reachable only with the IAM permissions in §9. DynamoDB keeps the item records (key lookups, always-scope index).
    - **No reranker:** Amazon Rerank and Cohere Rerank are not offered in Stockholm; calling Frankfurt would add 150–300 ms and send snippets out of Stockholm.
    - **Direct ingestion** (`IngestKnowledgeBaseDocuments`) per item instead of data-source syncs, so items index in parallel instead of queuing behind one sync job; enrichment runs alongside it (Step Functions Parallel). A full sync remains a manual repair only (AWS says not to run both at once).
    - **Lambdas:** api 1,024 MB with **SnapStart** (~$4/month per GB cached: about $4/month), cutting cold starts from ~1–2 s to a few hundred ms; knowledge Lambda 2,048 MB, reserved concurrency 50; heavy libraries imported lazily; one botocore client per process with a 50-connection pool, keep-alive and adaptive retries. Provisioned concurrency rejected: it only keeps a set number warm (SnapStart covers bursts) and SnapStart can't be combined with it.
    - **Caching:** a 60 s per-process cache of search results (item records always re-read); nothing else, since the query embedding happens inside Bedrock and the prompt cache already holds the stable prompt.
67. **Knowledge v2 infra details (2026-11-07).**
    - **The OpenSearch index is created with the `opensearch-project/opensearch` provider** (`opensearch_index`, SigV4 service `aoss`, the deploy role's credentials, URL = the collection endpoint). Neither hashicorp/aws (6.72) nor awscc (1.109) has an OpenSearch Serverless index resource; a local-exec `curl` would need its own signing. The deploy role gets a data-access rule for `CreateIndex`/`UpdateIndex`/`DescribeIndex` on `items` only (the provider reads the index back with Describe); a `time_sleep` of 60 s lets the data-access policy apply first. Bedrock adds fields to the mapping, so Terraform ignores mapping drift. The provider skips its version ping (`opensearch_version` set), which OpenSearch Serverless doesn't answer.
    - **The collection's network policy is public** (dashboards off), so the GitHub runner can create the index; every call still needs IAM SigV4 and the data-access policy. With `AllowFromPublic` a Bedrock source-service rule is not needed.
    - **SnapStart stays on arm64:** AWS lists Python 3.12+ SnapStart for x86_64 and arm64. The api function publishes a version on every deploy and API Gateway invokes the `live` alias. Its ephemeral storage stays 512 MB (SnapStart's limit).
    - S3 Vectors, its KMS key-policy entry and its boundary actions are removed; `StartIngestionJob`/`GetIngestionJob` are replaced by the direct-ingestion actions; the boundary allows `aoss:APIAccessAll` and the three `neptune-graph:*DataViaQuery` actions (each role's own policy limits them to the one collection or graph).

68. **A native iPhone app with no SDKs (2026-11-07).** The owner asked for an iOS app that does what the HUD does. It is SwiftUI in `ios/` with no third-party packages: Cognito sign-in calls Cognito's public JSON API directly (SRP-6a in Swift, checked against Python big-integer vectors in which a fake server derives the same key; first-login password, TOTP setup and codes, forgot password; refresh with `GetTokensFromRefreshToken`, because the app client has refresh-token rotation on; sign-out with `RevokeToken`). It uses the **same app client** as the web HUD (`ALLOW_USER_SRP_AUTH` only), because SRP needs no callback URL or client secret, so **no infra change** is needed. Amplify Swift was not used: it would be a large dependency, and its refresh path with rotation is not proven. Tokens are in the Keychain (`WhenUnlockedThisDeviceOnly`). The app reads the same public `config.json` from the site (address typed once, or `AIC_SITE_HOST` in `ios/Config/AICity.xcconfig`).
69. **Voice on the iPhone uses Apple's speech APIs, not Transcribe and Polly (2026-11-07).** Push-to-talk uses the Speech framework (on-device recognition when the phone supports it) and replies are read by `AVSpeechSynthesizer`. Calling Transcribe streaming from Swift needs SigV4 and AWS's event-stream framing over WebSocket with identity-pool credentials, which is a lot of hand-written code to keep correct; Apple's APIs need no AWS credentials on the phone, cost nothing and keep the audio on the phone when possible. The trade-offs: no custom "AIC" vocabulary (the app passes "AIC" as a contextual hint instead), the voice is the system voice rather than Polly's, and hands-free wake mode is not built on iOS (push-to-talk only).
70. **The iPhone app's City is the web HUD's own scene (the owner's choice, 2026-11-07).** The owner wants the phone's City to look exactly like the browser's, so the app does not redraw it: `web/src/city/scene.js` and `city-state.js` are built into one small page (`web/city-embed.html`, `npm run build:city-embed -w @aic/web`) and bundled in the app as `ios/AICity/CityWeb/`. A `WKWebView` shows it, locked down: pages come only from the app bundle through a private `aic-city://` scheme that serves just those two files, every other navigation is refused, the page's CSP forbids any network access, the data store is non-persistent, and there are no pop-ups or link previews. The page never sees a token: the app passes it the `/city` snapshot and each WebSocket event as plain values, and the page reports back only what was tapped (`{type, id}`), checked before use. A bundled build was chosen over loading the deployed HUD so no Cognito tokens have to be put into a web page and the City works offline from the last snapshot. A Node test (`tests/city-embed.test.ts`) fails when the scene sources change without rebuilding the bundle, so the two cannot drift apart. Pinch, pan and taps are the scene's own; the web view's page zoom and scrolling are off. Approvals in the app need a 1.2 s hold on a sheet that shows the cost line; VoiceOver users approve with a double-tap on that sheet's Approve element. Supersedes the first, natively drawn City.
71. **Apps the city builds are served from their own origin inside a sandboxed iframe (the owner's request, 2026-11-07).** A game or small feature asked of AIC is built by the Apps department as a self-contained web app and published to a separate bucket (`aic-prod-apps`) behind a second CloudFront distribution with `connect-src 'none'`; the HUD shows it only in `<iframe sandbox="allow-scripts">` (opaque origin: no cookies, no storage, no API), and never listens to its messages. Agent-written code can't reach the owner's tokens even if it tried. The apps CSP's `frame-ancestors` is `https://*.cloudfront.net`, because naming the HUD's exact domain would make the two distributions depend on each other; a custom domain removes that residual risk. Contract: `docs/APPS.md`.
72. **Publishing an app needs the owner, decided by the API, not by an agent (2026-11-07).** Workers submit files to their own workspace prefix (`submit_app`); the apps Lambda validates them (index.html, size, path and type allowlist; content types set by the city) and stages a preview; on the Orchestrator's PASS sign-off the city code (not the prompt) files an `app.publish` approval with the preview. Approving in Needs you makes the owner-only API copy the draft to `live/`; no agent tool can publish or remove an app. Workers and the Orchestrator get no new permissions.
73. **Apps can read the owner's data through a granted, read-only bridge (the owner's requirement, 2026-11-07).** The owner wants to add anything on demand, for example a combined calendar page with a top-bar icon, built as an app rather than a fixed screen. An app's manifest asks for capabilities from one fixed catalogue (calendar, mail search, read-only connector tools); the owner grants them at publish in Needs you and can revoke them later. At runtime the app asks the HUD with `postMessage`; the HUD accepts only messages from the frame it mounted, answers only granted capabilities, and calls an owner-only API route that runs the read through the policy gate as role `app`. The app never sees a token, the API address or the owner's session, and keeps `connect-src 'none'`. Supersedes the "never listens to its messages" part of decision 70. Contract: `docs/APPS.md` § v2.
74. **Apps never act for the owner; they can only propose (2026-11-07).** Nothing that sends, deletes, accepts or changes anything is grantable. An app may propose one of a short list of actions (answer an invitation, create an event, draft a mail); each proposal is a normal approval in Needs you with the same preview as when AIC asks, run once by the API on approve. Pinned apps (up to 6) sit as icons in the top bar.
75. **Connectors are data: any MCP server, added by asking AIC, with sign-in when needed (the owner's requirement, 2026-11-07).** The owner says *"add higgsfield as an MCP"*. AIC's `add_connector` tool (offered only on turns started by an owner message) resolves the URL, or the name through the official MCP Registry, whose content is untrusted. It then files an owner approval bound to the exact URL and roles. Only the owner's yes provisions it. The city then probes the server. OAuth goes through AgentCore Identity: dynamic client registration when offered, a "Sign in to …" button in the chat, a callback on the HUD, and the token stored in the vault under the fixed user `owner`. An API key goes into a secure HUD field that writes to Secrets Manager and an Identity API-key provider; keys are never pasted into chat. Tools work on the next turn without a redeploy. GitHub, Tavily (the owner's search choice; AgentCore's Web Search Tool isn't in eu-north-1) and Slack are added the same way. This replaces the earlier fixed Terraform list (v1, same day). Contract: `docs/CONNECTORS_SKILLS.md`.
76. **Every connector is a Gateway Lambda target on one bridge Lambda, and the Cedar policies are city data.** With a bridge, one owner sign-in serves all four agent roles. Native authorization-code Gateway targets don't document how the user is identified under `AWS_IAM` inbound auth, and are likely per caller. Only the bridge reads credentials, from the Identity vault at call time; runtimes hold only `InvokeGateway`. Checks come in three layers. IAM decides who may call the Gateway. Cedar in AgentCore Policy (`ENFORCE`) sets the ceiling per role; the connectors Lambda rewrites these policies after each change, and Terraform owns the engine only. The runtime filters per role and per worker (`WorkerSpec.connectors`), and every call passes `policy.gate` and the audit as `mcp__gateway__<id>___<tool>`, with destructive tools on "ask" by default and output treated as untrusted data. The SDK path keeps `allowed_tools=[]`. Provisioning is an AWS change made by city code (the connectors Lambda) only after the owner approves, never by an agent. Residual risk: per-worker limits hold only in the runtime, because workers share one role.
77. **Skills are owner-added `SKILL.md` folders in S3, fetched at turn start.** They live in bucket `aic-prod-skills`, with one ledger row per skill (`SKILLS` / `SKILL#<name>`) and a version flip on each replace. SDK agents load them as a city-built local plugin (`--plugin-dir`) and never through the SDK's `skills` option, because that option pre-approves `Skill(name)` past `can_use_tool` and turns setting sources back on. `setting_sources=[]` stays, and `can_use_tool` checks every `Skill` call against the role's set. AIC gets a `use_skill` city tool instead. Uploads keep only the `name` and `description` frontmatter: `allowed-tools`, `hooks` and the rest are dropped, because they would get past the gate. Agents can't write skills. Scripts run only through a worker's gated, environment-scrubbed `Bash`.
78. **Mail and calendar are city tools that use AgentCore Identity, not Gateway targets (2026-11-07).** The owner asked AIC to take full control over several mailboxes and calendars from different providers. Google and Microsoft sign in through AgentCore Identity OAuth2 credential providers (USER_FEDERATION; the token vault keeps and refreshes the tokens), and IMAP/CalDAV app passwords are kept in Secrets Manager (`aic/prod/mail/<id>`), entered only in a secure HUD form. The tools (`mcp__city__mail_*`, `calendar_*`) call the providers directly, because IMAP/SMTP aren't HTTP and a Gateway target holds one token per inbound identity, while the owner may connect two addresses at one provider. So each account is its own vault user (`userId` = account id). The sign-in URL never passes through the model: the HUD fetches it when the owner taps "Sign in to …". This is the same auth system as the MCP connectors (decision 67), and mail accounts are a built-in connector kind: AIC adds them through `add_connector`, and they're listed under Settings → Connectors. Contract: `docs/MAIL_CALENDAR.md` (part of the connectors contract since 2026-11-07). Numbered 74–76 because the apps branch uses 70–73.
79. **Full control, with the owner in charge of what leaves or is destroyed.** Reading, searching, drafting, labelling and archiving are allowed. Sending, trashing, answering invitations, inviting people and deleting events are "ask" in `config/policy.json`, and Needs you shows the whole email or the event before approval. Only two standing rules can pre-approve: `send_to` (named recipients or domains, from one account) and `accept_from` (accepting invitations from named organizers when there is no conflict). Only the owner can create them, through the API. A rule answers the gate's "ask" and is audited; it never changes the policy. Permanent delete exists in no tool. Mail and event text is wrapped as untrusted data. Access: AIC has everything; the Orchestrator and managers get read only when the owner turns it on; **workers never** get it (their runtime role is shared and can run shell commands, so IAM keeps tokens away from it).
80. **No hardcoded Calendar page; a combined calendar is an app (the owner's choice, 2026-11-07).** The separate mail and calendar feature was shelved and folded into connectors. The combined calendar the owner wants is built on request by the apps feature (`docs/APPS.md`) and reads every connected calendar through the read-only bridge (`MAIL_CALENDAR.md` §11, role `aic-prod-fn-app-bridge`). App-proposed writes go to Needs you. The HUD has no `/calendar/*` routes and no Calendar screen. Replaces the mail branch's earlier decision 72 (a combined Calendar screen), which was never built.
81. **Any MCP server, on demand, including package (stdio) servers (the owner's requirement, 2026-11-07).** There's no list: a connector exists only after the owner asks AIC for it. Remote servers go through the bridge (decision 68). npm and PyPI servers that normally run locally run in one generic AgentCore Runtime, `aic-prod-mcp-host`, with one microVM session per connector. It installs the exact version the owner approved and runs it over stdio, with a scrubbed environment and no AWS permissions; it gets the connector's own variables from the bridge in memory. The approval card names the package, the exact version, the publisher and the source, and an update is a new approval. Docker packages and in-process browser logins aren't supported. Residual risk: the package is third-party code with the owner's key for that one service and internet access, but no reach into AWS, the city or other connectors. Contract: `docs/CONNECTORS_SKILLS.md` §1.10.
82. **Web APIs without an MCP server connect through their OpenAPI document (the owner's choice, 2026-11-07).** The owner gives an OpenAPI 3 link. The city turns each operation into a tool: GET is read-only, DELETE is destructive, and everything else is a write. Auth comes from the spec's security schemes: an API key or bearer token in the secure field, OAuth authorization code through AgentCore Identity with the owner's own client, or client credentials. The bridge Lambda makes the calls, so every connector shares one path, one Cedar ceiling and one gate. It isn't a native Gateway OpenAPI target, for the same identity reason as decision 68. Calls are pinned to the spec's https host. GraphQL, SOAP, gRPC and file uploads aren't supported. Contract: `docs/CONNECTORS_SKILLS.md` §1.11.
83. **AIC's container is kept warm (the owner's under-3-seconds target, 2026-11-07).** The first message after a deploy or an idle quarter-hour paid the AgentCore container's start, several times AIC's own reply time. Now the scheduler sends a `{"kind": "warm"}` invocation every 5 minutes on exactly the session id the dispatcher uses for the owner's conversation (`warm_ace`, enabled from the start, unlike the other jobs), the runtime answers it before any ledger or model work, AIC's session may live 8 hours with a 30-minute idle window, and the deploy warms AIC once right after `terraform apply`. AgentCore bills active processing only, so a warm idle container costs nothing. After the 8-hour lifetime the ping itself pays the next start, not the owner.
84. **Scheduled jobs run from the start (2026-11-07).** Decision 33 created every EventBridge Scheduler job DISABLED for the owner to switch on by hand. The owner asked for every feature to be finished and running, so Terraform now enables the crash sweep, idle-agent retirement, reminders, nudges and the AIC warm ping (`state = "ENABLED"` per job). The weekly self-improvement job stays DISABLED, as decision 34 and the rules require.
85. **The iPhone app signs in once (the owner's request, 2026-11-07).** "Sign in once, then just open the app, even weeks later." The tokens, including Cognito's refresh token, stay in the Keychain with `AfterFirstUnlockThisDeviceOnly` (readable after the phone's first unlock since it started, so a launch iOS prepares early still finds them; never synced or backed up), written in place so a failed write never loses them; items from the first version move to this rule on first read. On launch and every time the app comes to the front it refreshes the ID and access tokens with `GetTokensFromRefreshToken` if they are within a minute of expiring, stores the rotated refresh token each time, reconnects the WebSocket at once and reloads the city. One refresh runs at a time and only the refresh itself clears that slot; a refresh refused because another one rotated the token meanwhile retries with the newer token. Only a refresh token Cognito refuses (revoked, or past its validity, which the web session raises to 365 days) shows the sign-in screen; no connection never does. Debug builds have `-demo -demo-cognito` (a fake Cognito with 2-minute ID tokens) to check quitting and relaunching in the simulator.
86. **AIC always on (the owner's measurement, 2026-11-07).** With the warm-keeper live the owner still saw 8–9 s to the first word after a quiet spell, 5 s on the next message and 3 s from the third on. The container was up, but the first turn paid for lazy clients and TLS (Bedrock, the HUD WebSocket, DynamoDB), the model's cold first token, and the dispatcher's and ws-broadcast's Lambda cold starts. So: the runtime creates its Bedrock client at start; each warm ping (now every 2 minutes, and after every deploy) readies the sender for the HUD's connections, reads the ledger and makes a one-token model call in the background (about 700 calls a day, a few cents); and the dispatcher and ws-broadcast run with provisioned concurrency 1 on a published `live` alias (SQS and EventBridge target the alias). Cost about $14/month. The api Lambda keeps SnapStart; SnapStart and provisioned concurrency can't share a function. Still serverless: nothing to patch or scale by hand.
87. **AIC does simple requests itself; a Haiku triage decides, not a Fable round trip (2026-11-07).** The owner wants quick answers ("50 sentences on …") from AIC on the spot and only real work sent to the team, with the Orchestrator deciding whether to use an existing department or create one. A full Orchestrator (Fable) turn just to decide costs seconds, so the decision is the Orchestrator's *quick triage*: one short utility-model call inside `delegate_to_orchestrator`, cached per request and costed like other utility turns. `self` means no task is created and AIC answers in full in the same reply; otherwise the task is delegated exactly as before with the triage's department hint, and the Orchestrator keeps the final say. Any triage failure delegates.
88. **A hard cap on workers per goal (2026-11-07).** A 50-sentence writing request produced a Research & Writing department with about 21 workers; the owner: "why are there so many agents working on that? That's really bad, fix it." Prompts alone had allowed it ("there is no limit on how many workers you hire"). Now `max_workers_per_goal` (3) and a lower per-department ceiling (10) are enforced in `hire_worker` itself, not only asked of the model; managers hire one worker per deliverable, and the Orchestrator never creates a department for a one-off question (AIC answers those itself after decision 87's triage).
89. **AIC can look at one job and stop it, in plain words (2026-11-07).** After a misrouted request, the owner asked AIC how it was going and got "I can't see that job's progress from here… 29 tickets running… $1.95 of your $10,000 cap": no per-job view, no way to stop it, and jargon (there is no cap, only a budget email). Now `get_task_status` finds a job by the owner's own words (the task stores `owner_words`) and describes it plainly; `cancel_task` confirms with one line, then cancels every open part, releases the workers and makes the dispatcher drop queued work for it (the runtime also skips cancelled work). The status summary speaks of jobs and says when no limit is set. AIC's prompt forbids inside words (ticket, cap, ledger, orchestrator), asks one question at a time, and ends running-work replies with the stop offer, so a seven-year-old could drive it.
90. **No email; one iPhone push per finished job (the owner's words, 2026-11-07).** "I don't need an email per finished ticket or task… remove emails fully… the only thing I need is a notification from the iOS app when a full feature is completed." So: the SNS email subscription and every email path are removed (the `notify` topic stays only as the CloudWatch alarm action, with no subscriber); the notifier is wired to `ticket.status` events filtered by EventBridge to top-level jobs (`kind: task`) reaching `done`/`failed`/`cancelled`, and a ledger claim per job makes it exactly one push, sent to every registered phone through SNS mobile push (APNs token key in `aic/prod/apns`, platform applications created only once `apns_enabled` is on, so deploys never need the key). Parts, reviews, hires and partial results never notify.
91. **The iPhone gets the push, the app only registers (the owner's request, 2026-11-07).** "The only thing I need is to get a notification from the iOS app when a full feature is completed… Not small features, larger." The backend (decision 90) sends one push per finished top-level job through SNS mobile push (APNs); the app's whole job is to ask, register and open. It asks once, right after the first sign-in (after the first-run guide when that shows), with one plain sentence before iOS's own prompt; "Not now" is never asked again, and Settings → Notifications can turn it on later or points to iOS Settings when it was refused there. When allowed, the app registers with APNs on every launch and return to the foreground (Apple's advice: the token can change) and sends the token to `POST /devices` with `platform: "ios"` and `sandbox` read from the provisioning profile's `aps-environment` (simulator and Xcode builds get the APNs sandbox, TestFlight and the App Store production). The backend's device id is kept in UserDefaults (it is not a secret; the tokens stay in the Keychain); a changed token replaces the old device, sign-out calls `DELETE /devices/{id}` while the tokens still work, turning notifications off in iOS Settings removes the device on the next open, and a removal that fails offline is finished later. The notification's words (job, outcome, cost) are written by the backend; the app shows them as given and a tap opens the AIC tab. The Push Notifications entitlement needs the Apple Developer Program, so it sits behind `AIC_ENTITLEMENTS` in `ios/Config/AICity.xcconfig` (on for the simulator, empty by default for devices) and a free personal team still builds; `PushController.failed` explains this in Settings instead of failing silently.
92. **Workers go home on their own (the owner's request, 2026-11-07).** "For each agent that gets done, they can just go home, with an animation just like when it got to work." The scene already animated released workers; the backend seldom released them (managers forget `release_worker`, and `retire_idle` only slept managers), so 21 workers sat at desks after a paused job. Now `move` releases an assignment's worker as soon as its last open assignment reaches done, failed or cancelled, through the factory's release (one `agent.retired`, idempotent), and the sweep sends home any worker idle for `worker_idle_release_minutes` (15) with no turn under a live lease, paused city included: a seated idle worker does nothing and a released one costs nothing. Its unfinished assignments are cancelled with a note and the manager gets a message to assign again within the cap, rather than leaving a queued turn to hit a retired worker and stall the part.
93. **"Send the workers home" is its own control, and never a pause (the owner's words, 2026-11-07).** "Whenever I say send the workers home, the city gets paused… the response should be 'are you sure about that?' and then I say yes… the workers will be sent home and drop everything." AIC's only blunt tool was `pause_city`, so it reached for it. Now `send_workers_home` releases every live worker (the City animates them walking home), cancels their open parts with a note and tells each manager once; the city keeps running. The prompt names the four controls (cancel one job, send everyone home, pause, resume), each confirmed once; pausing only on an explicit "pause everything" / "stop the city".
94. **"Send everyone home" is instant and never needs a model (the owner's business requirement, 2026-11-07).** The owner: "they should be able to be sent back home whenever I tell them to… drop everything and just go home. That's mandatory." Telling AIC did nothing because AIC's runtime released workers through the Agent Factory Lambda, which only the manager role may invoke (the unit tests used fakes and never saw the AccessDenied). Releasing is a ledger write and one `agent.retired` event, so AIC and the API now do it in-process (`Factory`); hiring stays with the managers and the Lambda. The HUD gets a "Send everyone home" button on a direct route (`POST /workers/home`) and a `POST /tasks/{id}/cancel`, so the owner never waits on AIC → Orchestrator → manager. The idle sweep no longer trusts a lease longer than a turn may take, counts a never-active worker from its hire time, runs the crash sweep first, and skips (and logs) a worker it cannot release instead of aborting.
95. **The city is always on; pausing is gone (the owner, 2026-11-07).** "I don't know why there is still something called resume work and city paused. Remove the feature. The city should always be on, there's no point in pausing the city." The pause flag had become a trap: AIC reached for it whenever the owner said anything like "stop", and a paused city looked broken (workers seated, nothing moving). Removed end to end: the AIC tools, the routes, the setting, the `city.state` event, the dispatcher and turn-gate checks, the HUD buttons, banners and Settings section. The only city-wide stop is `send_workers_home` (instant, one confirmation); one job is stopped with `cancel_task`; spend caps still hold at dispatch and in the turn gate without any paused state. `scripts/city-off.sh` keeps only the queue-trigger switch for the owner's cost-saving use.
96. **AIC speaks with OpenAI's voice (the owner's choice, 2026-11-07).** "Use OpenAI's voice, whatever they have as default." Polly's generative voices still sounded synthetic to the owner. OpenAI text-to-speech (`gpt-4o-mini-tts`, default voice `alloy`) is a voice service only: every agent keeps running on Anthropic models through Bedrock, and the key (`aic/prod/openai`, pasted in Settings → Voice) is readable by the api function alone, never by agents, the browser or the logs. The HUD asks `POST /speech` per sentence and gets MP3 bytes; until the key is in, or when OpenAI fails, it falls back to Polly. Cost: about $0.015 per 1,000 characters.
97. **Scheduled jobs run as EventBridge rules, not EventBridge Scheduler schedules (2026-11-07).** In the lab account the Scheduler never reached the Lambda: 110 invocation attempts in two hours, every one a `TargetErrorCount` and `InvocationDroppedCount`, while the function logged only the deploy's own warm-up calls, although the scheduler role's `lambda:InvokeFunction` simulated as allowed under its boundary. Role-based service invocations are refused somewhere outside IAM policy (most likely the organisation's policies). Classic EventBridge rules invoke through the function's resource policy, which the city-event rules already prove works here, so every job (sweep, retire-idle, reminders, warm-ace; `improve` stays DISABLED) is now an `aws_cloudwatch_event_rule` with a `schedule_expression`, a target carrying the job input, and an `aws_lambda_permission` for `events.amazonaws.com`. The Scheduler group, its role and policy are gone.
98. **The team obeys the owner, and only the owner (2026-11-07).** The owner: "when I say send everyone home… they're still at work… make sure they listen to what I say… I don't want a button." What had happened: a complaint about the voice became three departments and over a hundred workers, and after a send-home the managers re-hired because the jobs stayed open. Now: a job starts only on the owner's explicit yes (AIC asks one question first; the tool creates nothing without `confirm=true`); feedback, complaints or questions about AI City itself are never jobs (they are made in the owner's builder session); "send everyone home" stops every job, sends everyone home and stands the managers down until the owner starts a new job; a manager's question is answered only with the owner's own words, never a default AIC fills in; and hard ceilings (`max_live_workers_city` 10, `max_open_jobs` 3, `max_departments` 6, editable in Settings) hold even if every prompt fails. The HUD button is gone: the owner talks to AIC.
99. **Start over is a Settings button, not a terminal script (2026-11-07).** The owner asked for the whole application to be reset while on the phone, where the Mac's terminal (and its MFA prompt) cannot be reached. A reset the owner cannot run is not idiot-proof, so it is part of the product: Settings → Start over, a held button, `POST /city/reset` with `confirm: true`. It removes the city's work and purges the queues, keeps everything the owner set up (settings, spend history, devices, connectors, skills, knowledge, apps, mail, notes, reminders, specialists), and AIC and the Orchestrator come back on the next message. The api role gets `dynamodb:Scan` and `dynamodb:BatchWriteItem` on the ledger and `sqs:PurgeQueue` on the four queues for exactly this; no agent role has them.
100. **No guardrails on the owner's work (2026-11-08).** The owner: "I want AIC and the agents without any guardrails; it should do exactly anything I want, like my own Claude would, with the AWS models as the only difference". The policy gate keeps running on every call (the plumbing for audit and for apps), but its rules shrink to what keeps the city alive: no agent changes the city's own AWS account (IAM, budgets, secrets, KMS, resources), runs a deploy or image push, reads a credential file or keychain, or wipes the sandbox root; secrets never flow through the Gateway; apps keep their granted reads. Everything about the owner's own work (merging, pushing, GitHub, mail, publishing, payments, SQL, installs) is allowed outright and nothing waits for an approval. The builder keeps a sentence of concern on record: a worker with IAM or Terraform rights could lock the owner out of the account, which is why those rules stay unless the owner removes them too.
101. **Any single worker can be sent home, from the City or through AIC (2026-11-08).** The owner: "I should be able to click any agent or worker and send him back home, but also tell AIC to send back a specific person or everyone or however many I want." Both paths do the same thing with no model deciding anything: the clicked worker (or the ones AIC's `send_home` picked from the owner's words: id, department, role, number, everyone) has its open parts cancelled with a note and is released, so the City walks it home. Its job and manager are left alone and nobody is hired in its place; the owner starts a new job if they want the work done. AIC asks once and acts on a clear yes, like the other two controls. "Working now" in the top bar counts everyone drawn at a desk, so it can never say 0 while someone is seated.
102. **A reset works in rounds and a send-home never half-succeeds silently (2026-11-08).** The owner held Start over on a city of 400 agents and 311 jobs; the single scan-and-delete outran the API Lambda's 30 s, so three departments and one worker stayed while the HUD reported an error. The reset now answers within 18 s per round with `done`, and the HUD keeps asking until it is done, with a progress line. A worker is released even when cancelling one of its tickets fails (half-deleted data must never keep a figure at a desk), and the single-worker route refuses (500) rather than claim success when the release failed. The owner's rule restated: every figure at a desk is one agent; "everyone home" sends every one of them home and leaves only the managers, the Orchestrator and AIC.
103. **Glass around the City, the arrow pointer everywhere (2026-11-08).** The owner: "the pointer should always be the regular pointer, even over clickable things; instead the clickable thing should react, like a change of opacity" and "redesign the entire theme to a glassy design; the City's UI and colours stay the same." So: one stylesheet, two worlds. Everything around the City is translucent dark glass over a lit night sky in the same cream/ink/amber palette (blur only on containers: the top bar, dialogs, cards, the chat sheet; rows and chips only tint, so a long list never stutters on a phone). The City view's section of the stylesheet is untouched, and the shared pieces it draws (buttons, banners, the empty card) are overridden back to parchment inside `.city`. No element anywhere shows the hand cursor or the grab hand; pressable things fade to 78% on hover and 62% while pressed, and the fade is skipped under reduced motion. Reduced transparency makes the glass solid night. The iPhone app's native screens are the next step (materials), kept as its own task.
104. **AIC is written AIC and said "Ace" (2026-11-08).** The owner: "I know it is AIC, but I want it pronounced Ace; it should answer to hey Ace, yo Ace, Ace, with the spelling AIC." This reverses the earlier "never Ace" rule. Spoken replies say "Ace" wherever the text says AIC (web `speakable`, the iPhone's speech normaliser), the wake word accepts `ace` as the name on both halves with the shared fixture updated (a sentence ending in "ace" counts as addressing it; one in the middle goes to Haiku), the wake-word prompt and AIC's own prompt say the name is written AIC and said Ace so AIC never corrects the owner, and every screen keeps the spelling AIC.

105. **AIC's voice is marin styled after ChatGPT's Sol, not Sol itself (2026-11-08).** The owner asked for "Sol, the ChatGPT voice, the exact same". ChatGPT's nine app voices (Arbor, Breeze, Cove, Ember, Juniper, Maple, Sol, Spruce, Vale) exist only inside ChatGPT: no OpenAI API lists them, there is no file to download, and OpenAI's custom-voice feature clones only a consenting speaker's own recording, so an MP3 of Sol cannot be turned into a voice. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore has no OpenAI voice either (AI City's OpenAI use is the speech key alone). The closest the speech API allows: `marin` (one of the two voices OpenAI recommends for quality) with an `instructions` line that asks for Sol's manner, savvy and relaxed; `tts-1`, the fallback model, has no instructions and no marin, so it speaks with `sage`. Whether marin sounds right is the owner's call: every other voice is one pick away in Settings → Voice. OpenAI has announced that `gpt-4o-mini-tts` and `tts-1` shut down on 2027-01-06 in favour of the Realtime API, which is a different workflow; that migration is a job for later in 2026.

106. **The + menu adds nothing to the backend (2026-11-08).** The owner wanted Claude's composer menu in the chat: files or photos, folders, slash commands, connectors, plugins. Files attached to a message are uploaded to the knowledge base, the one place the agents can already read files from, and the message names them in an "Attached (now in my knowledge base): …" line that AIC's prompt tells it to search for first; the files stay in Knowledge afterwards (a feature, not a leak: everything the owner shows AIC is theirs to find again). Slash commands expand in the browser into the plain sentences AIC already understands, so AIC sees no new syntax and no tool changed. Plugins are the skills under a name the owner knows from Claude. The chip's × deletes the knowledge item it made, since the owner changed their mind before AIC saw it.

107. **A small eye beside the voice line, not a big drawing (2026-11-08).** The owner: "remove the large eye… something more discreet and more focused on the chat; keep the voice effect". The drawing shrinks to 44 px at the start of the voice line, so the eye still names AIC's state for assistive tech and the line keeps its bars, wave and dots; the voice column gets slimmer and the chat wider. Nothing else on the screen moved.

108. **The chat runs behind the top bar (2026-11-08).** The owner: "the chatbot shouldn't say Chat with AIC in the top bar of the chat; it should blend in behind the tabs bar, faded and glassy, with the bar the most focused part; and remove the eye next to AI City". On the AIC screen only, the top bar floats over the page and the chat panel starts at the very top, so messages scroll up under the bar's blur; the panel's header is gone (the clear button floats in its corner) and the first message starts below the bar. Other screens keep the bar in the flow: their content is lists and forms that should not hide under it. The brand is the AI CITY wordmark everywhere; the eye drawing now appears only as the small state eye on the AIC screen and the status row.

109. **The iPhone's chat gets the browser's + menu, behind-the-bar layout and wordmark, with two phone-sized differences (2026-11-08).** The owner: "Yes, iPhone too." The native app mirrors decisions 106–108 word for word: the same five menu items, the same slash commands expanding to the same sentences (`ios/AICity/Core/Attach.swift` is a port of `web/src/lib/attach.ts`, with the browser's tests ported to XCTest), files uploaded through the same knowledge routes and named in the same "Attached (now in my knowledge base): …" line, the conversation running up under the glass top bar with the AI CITY wordmark as its title, the eye shrunk to 44 pt at the start of the voice line, and the brand rows showing the wordmark alone. Two things differ because a phone is not a browser: "Add files or photos" asks Photos or Files first (iOS keeps them in two pickers), and "Add a connector…" writes `/connect ` in front of the words instead of "Add ___ as a connector" with the blank selected, since a SwiftUI text box cannot select a range; the sentence AIC receives is the same. "Add a plugin…" says skills are added on the web HUD, which has the Skills tab. Speak replies becomes the browser's three-way pill (When I talk / Always / Never; the old on/off switch's "off" becomes Never). Hands-free listening is still not on iOS (decision 69): the toggle the browser gained has no iPhone counterpart, and none was invented.

110. **Full GitHub access and a terminal for AIC (the owner's choice: no guardrails on the owner's own GitHub, 2026-11-08).** The owner: "I really don't care about safety and security; I just want full access to my GitHub account: create any new repository, any new organization, whatever; AIC needs terminal access to run git push, git commit, git add in a shell; it all runs inside AgentCore; and I need internet access to browse other stuff as well." Until now workers had git but no credentials, so no `git push` could work, and AIC had no shell at all. Now one classic personal access token with every scope (`docs/GITHUB_ACCESS.md`) is pasted once in Settings → GitHub and kept in `aic/prod/github` (its own Terraform module; the empty container moved out of the storage module). Every AIC and worker runtime reads it at session start and signs git and `gh` in through the environment only: `GH_TOKEN`, `GITHUB_TOKEN`, a git credential helper for `https://github.com` that echoes the environment, and `AI City <id+login@users.noreply.github.com>` as the commit identity; nothing with the token in it is ever written to disk, and the token is blanked out of every tool result and report. AIC gets `run_command`: a shell in its own runtime microVM under `/workspace` (kept while the runtime lives, about 8 h), through the same deny rules as a worker's Bash (the city's AWS account, deploys, credential files, the sandbox root), 20 KB of output, 120 s by default; quick git and shell jobs the owner asks for are AIC's own now, real builds still go to the team (decision 87). The GitHub CLI is pinned in the image from GitHub's release .deb with its checksum. Trade-offs the owner accepted: a worker that goes wrong can push, force-push or delete anything the token reaches (the token is the owner's account, not a deploy key); the token lives in the memory of every AIC and worker microVM; GitHub's API cannot create an organization for a personal account (the owner makes orgs by hand once). The GitHub connector learns the same token: "GitHub" by name resolves to GitHub's own MCP server, a github.com page link gets a plain hint, a saved city token is used as the bearer key without a sign-in, and a sign-in card at github.com offers "I have a token instead". The dead Integrations switches in Settings (github / gmail / google calendar / spotify, read by nothing) are gone; the GitHub section takes their place.

111. **The iPhone app ships with its address built in; no Connect screen (the owner's choice, 2026-11-08).** The owner opened the app on their iPhone and met "Connect to your AI City", a form asking for the web address: "this should never happen when we open the app from the iphone, it should be install the app and that's it, you just have to login or register, that's it, no url pasting stuff." It is the owner's personal app for one deployment, so the address is a build setting, not a question: `AIC_SITE_HOST` in `ios/Config/AICity.xcconfig` holds the HUD's CloudFront host (Terraform's `hud_url` output, a public value), `Info.plist` carries it into the bundle and the app reads `config.json` from there, exactly as the web HUD does. The Connect screen, the typed address kept in UserDefaults and the "Change the AI City address" buttons are gone; the first screen is sign-in, or the first-login steps from the welcome email. A build whose setting is empty (a developer who skipped the xcconfig) fails with one plain sentence naming the setting, never a form. Demo mode never needed an address and is unchanged. Nothing in AWS changed.

112. **One voice button: a speech-to-speech conversation (2026-11-08).** The owner: "I just want one button to speak to it, like Claude: voice means speech to speech, I talk and then he talks, on until I click it off; transcribe is the other feature, then I hit send; remove the left side." The AIC screen keeps only the chat; the message bar gains a voice button beside Dictate. It replaces three controls (hands-free wake word, Speak replies, the big mic) whose combinations produced the silent replies the owner disliked. In a conversation every answer is spoken and the mic reopens after it; outside one, nothing is spoken (typed and dictated messages are text). Wake-word detection stays in the backend unused, so nothing in AWS changes.

113. **Two pre-configured departments, IT and Marketing, with skills and switchable connectors (2026-11-08).** The owner wanted ready-made departments like a company's, starting with IT and Marketing, each with Markdown skills for its field and the services it needs, switched on and off with the sign-in starting on the switch. They are config (`config/departments.json`), not records: a department appears in the City only when work arrives, as before, and the Orchestrator is steered to these names (aliases map onto them, so "Engineering" and "Design" no longer fork the work). Skills ship in the image and the Lambda bundle (`agents/skills/`), read-only, and join the owner's skills for that department's manager and workers. Connectors stay the owner's choice: a switch only adds a known server address through the existing connector path, so sign-in, roles, approvals and removal work as for any connector. Money is never moved by agents: ad campaigns are created paused, budgets are the owner's decision, and adding funds is done in the platform's billing page. Meta has no official MCP server; Pipeboard's is the one listed, with the owner's Meta sign-in. Next departments, when the owner asks: Sales, Customer support, Finance, Legal, People (HR), Product, Operations, Data.

114. **Nine departments, shared tools, essentials and a setup guide (2026-11-08).** The owner asked for Sales, Customer Support, Finance, Legal, Product, Operations and Data & Analytics to exist from the start next to IT and Marketing, with the tools most businesses use, tools shared across departments, and a guide to set everything up. Choices: (1) IT builds and runs software, reliability and security included; Data & Analytics is separate because answering business questions is a different job from running systems; Operations means business operations (projects, processes, automations, vendors, orders), not IT operations. (2) Shared tools (Stripe, Jira & Confluence, Slack, Notion, Zapier, files, scheduling, whiteboards, meeting notes) and shared skills belong to every department, so a ticket or a sale works the same everywhere; Stripe is one account for everything sold, products tagged per app. (3) "Essential" marks three or four tools per department that most businesses in that field use; the setup guide counts only those. (4) Only official hosted MCP servers that answered a live check are listed; services without one (Zendesk, QuickBooks, Mailchimp, Fortnox) are reached through Zapier rather than third-party wrappers holding the owner's credentials, except Meta and Google Ads through Pipeboard, the established option, because the platforms publish none. (5) Accounts are created by the owner on each service's own sign-up page (a link per tool); agents never create accounts, move money, sign or publish without the owner's yes. HR stays out: managers hire their own workers.

115. **Settings is a menu of pages, and there is a light appearance (2026-11-08).** The owner wanted Settings to navigate like an app's settings rather than one long page with every switch. Each topic is a page with its own address (`#/settings/<page>`) so the browser's Back and shared links work; the menu shows a title and one line per page; Save shows only where a page has fields to save. Appearance (System, Light, Dark) is a per-device choice applied before the first paint; Dark stays the default (decision 103's look), and Light keeps the same glass with ink text over a daytime sky. The browser's own `color-scheme` is left alone: setting it to dark recoloured plain links inside the City's parchment panels and failed the contrast audit.

116. **A Research department that ends in a business decision (2026-11-08).** The owner wants research (papers from the big labs and journals, what already makes money) turned into products to sell. Research is its own preset, separate from Product's user research: it scouts and sizes opportunities, Product decides what to build. Its output is fixed as a ranked shortlist with a one-page opportunity brief (customer, B2B or B2C, MVP, price through the one Stripe account, go-to-market, numbers, risks and licences, the decision asked), so the owner can say go in minutes; it builds nothing itself. Paper and web tools are official hosted servers only (Hugging Face, Exa, Consensus, OpenAlex and others), checked live; the built-in browser and fetch cover the rest, politely (public pages, robots.txt, no paywalls).

117. **Departments are fixed-size towers with automatic doors and a roof counter (2026-11-08).** The owner asked for tall New York-style buildings that never grow with the headcount, agents disappearing inside through automatic doors, and a counter on top. This replaces the shop-style offices with terraces of desks (decision on 2026-11-07's revert): the City no longer shows every worker at a desk, it shows how busy each department is (lit windows, the counter) and the commute in and out, and the panel of a tower lists who is inside. The plot height is fixed, so the map no longer reflows when a department hires. The counter follows arrivals and departures at the door rather than the ledger, so the number changes exactly when someone visibly goes in or out.

118. **Media is its own department, publishing through the owner's own scheduling tool (2026-11-08).** The owner wants videos made, edited, uploaded to YouTube, TikTok, Instagram and Facebook and monetized. Media is separate from Marketing because producing and running channels is daily production work, while Marketing plans campaigns and buys ads; they share Canva, Higgsfield, HeyGen and ElevenLabs. The social platforms publish no MCP servers, so uploads go through Metricool (first), Buffer, Ayrshare or Blotato, where the owner links each account once with the platform's own sign-in; the city never sees a social password. Monetization rules are in a skill that tells workers to check each platform's current thresholds rather than trust remembered numbers.

119. **The preset departments exist from the start; IT is three departments (2026-11-08).** The owner expected every department they had listed to stand in the City before any work. Departments are still ledger records (so Start over, the Orchestrator and the City treat them like any other), created idempotently when the City's snapshot is read (`ensure_presets`), which also brings them back after Start over. Managers are created with a department's first goal, not with the building, so "Working now" stays honest and the City is not full of idle people. IT is split into Application (building software) and IT Infrastructure (running and protecting it), with Data & AI as the third technical department, because those are different teams with different tools and skills; the old names are aliases. The department ceiling (decision 98) now applies only to departments beyond the presets, which are always allowed.
120. **AIC speaks in real time through Amazon Nova 2 Sonic, as an interface, not a second brain (2026-11-08).** The owner wanted AIC to sound and behave like a natural, real-time assistant: stop at once when talked over, add short human cues while it works, and keep the departments and orchestration as they are. Choices: (1) A second exception to "Anthropic models only": Nova 2 Sonic (`voice.speech` in `config/models.json`) only listens and speaks. Its one tool is `ask_aic`, which runs an ordinary AIC turn in-process; every fact, plan and task still comes from AIC and the Orchestrator. Its prompt (`agents/voice.md`) forbids answering from its own knowledge. (2) The relay lives in AIC's warm `ace` runtime at `/ws`, because browsers cannot open Bedrock's two-way stream. The API hands the browser a five-minute SigV4-signed WebSocket address to that runtime (`POST /voice/session`). Only the ace role may open the stream, only the API role may sign the address. (3) Barge-in: Sonic marks an interruption and the browser drops every queued sound at once. (4) Cues: after four seconds of AIC working, Sonic gets a `[status]` note and says a few words ("one moment, checking"), again every twelve seconds. (5) Bedrock caps a stream at eight minutes, so the relay moves to a fresh stream after about seven and replays the conversation. (6) Region availability is not certain, so the model list fails over (Stockholm, then Virginia, then Nova Sonic 1). (7) If Sonic cannot start, the step-by-step voice (Transcribe, AIC, OpenAI or Polly) takes over by itself.

121. **Lead research: a bundled Sales skill, the table in the chat and a CSV in Knowledge saved by the city (2026-11-08).** The owner wants "find me 2 leads" to be simple and fast: name, role, company, size, public work email, news, open roles and engineering roles, and who is hiring, from LinkedIn, Indeed and wherever jobs are posted, Swedish sources too. (1) It is Sales work: "business development", "bizdev", "leads", "lead generation", "prospecting" and "sales leads" are Sales aliases, so the Orchestrator's choice of name doesn't matter. (2) The how lives in `agents/skills/lead-research/SKILL.md`, not in code: defaults, sources in order (connectors that are on, then web search, then public pages and the JobTech API for Platsbanken), "not found publicly" with what was tried rather than a guess, exactly the asked fields. (3) The CSV is saved by plain code in AIC's runtime (`aic/shared/leads.py`) when the Orchestrator reports the job: it is the only role allowed to add to the knowledge base, it needs no new IAM, and no model retypes the rows. Each `csv` block is saved once per job (idempotency key), so a re-delivered report or a quoted copy adds nothing. (4) Limits, from the owner's "as long as it's legal": public pages and official APIs only; no logins as anyone else, no bypassing logins, paywalls or CAPTCHAs, no leaked data; no logged-in LinkedIn scraping (it gets the owner's account banned); the owner's own LinkedIn connector is fine. (5) Business contact details only. The owner later said GDPR is not a focus and asked for birth dates, home addresses and private phone numbers from Swedish lookup sites (Ratsit, Merinfo, Hitta, Eniro). That was not built: these are private details of named people, not business details, and putting them into an automated lead list goes beyond what this skill is for. The owner can still look a person up on those sites themselves. (6) Hunter (official hosted MCP, API key) joins Sales; LinkedIn has no official MCP server.

122. **Projects: every app is a project, in a studio of its own (2026-11-08).** The owner wanted the Apps tab to work like Lovable for non-technical people. Choices: (1) The nav item says Projects but the address stays `#/apps`, so pins, the City's cabinets and old links keep working. (2) A project's chat is its own conversation (`app-<app_id>`, or `app-new-<id>` before the app exists) and is kept out of AIC's main chat and voice; it lives on this device, like the main chat. (3) The preview is the same sandboxed frame as before, scaled with a CSS transform inside a fixed-size box, so an app sees a real 390 or 1440 px wide window; no new origin or CSP change. (4) "Go back to this version" for an older version calls the existing one-step rollback several times after a single confirm that names every version deleted, rather than a new API. (5) The timeline uses `GET /apps/<id>/activity` and falls back to the app events the HUD sees, so it never shows an error. (6) "Open the app" shows the app alone inside the HUD rather than in a new tab, so its granted data reads keep working.

123. **City → Features, and a built-in Calendar after all (2026-11-08).** The owner asked for a second tab inside City, "Features", where personal features live, the first one a Calendar that pulls in every meeting from every email account they connect. This reverses decision 85's "a combined calendar is an app, there is no Calendar page" for this one case: the owner asked for it by name, and a calendar that must import on a schedule, merge across accounts and answer AIC is city plumbing, not a sandboxed app. Choices: (1) The top bar is unchanged; City gets "City | Features" (`#/city`, `#/city/features`, `#/city/features/calendar`), the pill sitting in the City's stats card and on top of the Features pages. (2) A feature is a built-in part of the city the owner switches on or off (`FEATURES` rows in the ledger, Calendar on by default); "Add a feature" is a card that opens AIC's chat with "I'd like a feature that…", so a new one is asked for in words like everything else, and AIC builds it as a project. (3) The import runs in the existing `scheduler` Lambda (`calendar_import`, an EventBridge rule every 15 minutes like the other jobs, plus "Refresh now", which async-invokes the same job; one per 20 s); it reads through the existing `MailService` (Google, Microsoft, CalDAV) over 31 days back and 92 ahead, and for plain IMAP accounts it reads `.ics` invitations from INBOX read-only (EXAMINE, BODY.PEEK, nothing marked read) from the last UID it saw, keeping them in the ledger because each email is read once; repeating invitations are expanded in their own time zone, cancellations remove them. (4) The same meeting in several accounts is one: the same iCalendar UID and start, or the same title (ignoring "Fwd:", "Updated invitation:", case and punctuation), start and end with no different organizers; the merged meeting keeps every source account. (5) Meetings are ledger rows (`CALENDAR`/`EV#<start>#<id>`, TTL 120 days after the end), written only when they changed; an account that fails keeps its meetings until it answers again, a disconnected one loses them and its invitations. (6) Join links are pulled out by the backend (Teams, Zoom, Google Meet, Webex; https on the services' own meeting paths only, Outlook safe links unwrapped), and the HUD opens only https links, in a new tab without opener or referrer. (7) AIC gets `calendar_agenda`, which reads the ledger copy (fast, no provider call), answers in the owner's time zone (the HUD sends it with every Calendar load) and wraps titles as data from others; it goes through the policy gate like every city tool and has no write. Read-only by design: accepting, declining or changing a meeting stays with the mail tools and the owner. Cost: one small Lambda run every 15 minutes and a handful of DynamoDB writes when something changed, cents a month. Not tried against a real account.
    Update (2026-11-08): Nova 2.5 Sonic (`amazon.nova-2-5-sonic`, Stockholm, same price) became the first choice with Nova 2 Sonic behind it. The owner asked for a voice like ChatGPT's Sol. Sol is not offered outside ChatGPT, and Nova Sonic has 16 fixed voices with no custom or cloned voice; Amazon Polly Brand Voice is a separate paid engagement with AWS and is not speech-to-speech. So the voice stays `tiffany`, the closest match, and the personality (rhythm, emphasis, expressions, turn-taking) lives in `agents/voice.md`. WebRTC was considered and not adopted: it would change the transport, not the voice or the cues, and the browser already gets echo cancellation from getUserMedia.

124. **The Chief of Staff decides the owner's Needs you inbox (2026-11-08).** The owner wants nothing to pile up while they are away: a Fable 5.1 agent reviews every question and approval at once and "clicks it" for them, with no guardrails on their own work. Choices: (1) Fable 5.1 with Opus 5.5 as the one fallback, like the managers, as its own role `chief_of_staff` with an application inference profile, so the bill shows its cost. (2) No AgentCore runtime: the decision is one Converse call, so a Lambda (`chief-of-staff`, 5 min timeout) triggered by `question.asked` / `approval.requested`, plus a 5-minute sweep for anything missed or failed, is the smallest change. A ledger claim keeps it to one review per item per 10 minutes. (3) It never repeats the HUD's logic: it invokes the api Lambda's `live` alias with `{"chief_of_staff": op}`, which runs the very functions the HUD's buttons run (`decide_question`, `decide_approval`, `cancel_task`) and records the decision.
