HTTP events can't carry that key, and only the chief-of-staff role may invoke the api directly, so its own role stays small (ledger, bus, its model, that alias); publishing apps, Step Functions and connectors keep running on the api's role. (4) What an agent can't do (sign in, paste a key or password, pay, MFA) it never attempts: it picks an offered alternative, or leaves the item open with `needs_owner_reason` and is not asked again, so the inbox only ever holds those. (5) It may also stop a job stuck in a loop (it sees its own earlier decisions on that job). (6) Everything it decides is written on the item, kept 8 days under `STEWARD`, sent as `steward.decided` and listed by `GET /chief-of-staff`; a daily paragraph in AIC's chat (17:00 UTC) sums it up, written without a model call. (7) `chief_of_staff_enabled`, on by default; off = items wait for the owner as before. Agents are told "The owner answered…" as before, since the decision is the owner's by delegation. Approved actions still pass the policy gate when they run.

125. **Autopilot: nothing waits on the owner (2026-11-08).** The owner wants to be away for a month or two with every assigned task finished and customer tickets from Jira or Slack handled; Needs you "needs me too much". Choices: (1) A setting `autopilot`, on by default; off restores the earlier behaviour exactly. Under it the Chief of Staff is always on. (2) `ask_owner` still files a question (it is the record of the decision and the Chief's input) but no longer moves the part to `waiting_on_owner`; the Chief decides within seconds through the existing Lambda. Deciding inside the asking turn was rejected: it would give every runtime role the Chief's model and a second decision path. (3) The Chief must always end an item: after 3 tries without a usable reply or with errors it takes the default answer (a question) or refuses (an approval); a part with 5 decisions in a day is a loop, and its job is stopped without asking the model. The 3 Research items the owner saw were this case: an unanswerable "clean up by hand" request asked again and again. (4) What only the owner's hands can do (sign in, paste a key, pay, MFA) is never asked of anyone: the agent is told to carry on without it, and the owner gets one line per service on "When you're back" (`BACK`/<service>, updated in place, kept 62 days). The Chief never sees or types a credential. (5) Standing orders are ordinary jobs, not a new kind of agent: the scheduler starts one per due order (one live per order, default every 15 minutes), through the Orchestrator to the named department (Customer Support by default), using the city's existing connectors; `record_handled` keeps the handled items' keys (90 days) and the next round is told to skip them. Their rounds don't count against the owner's `max_open_jobs` and don't push to the iPhone; what they handled shows in `GET /away-summary`. They run whether or not Autopilot is on: each has its own on/off. (6) Decisions are kept 62 days (was 8) so a long absence still adds up. (7) Found while building: an approval decided before Step Functions stored its task token never woke the agent; now the decider re-reads the token after deciding and the approvals Lambda sends the result itself if the approval was already decided when it stores the token. Every tool call still passes the policy gate.

126. **AI workflows: known processes as data, confirmed before they run, and the owner's stated budget is the spend authorisation (2026-11-09).** The owner wants AIC to recognise when what they say matches a known process ("Is this what you mean: the Meta Ads workflow?"), then either walk them through it one page per step in the HUD or do it for them, asking only what is missing, one question at a time and never twice ("What's your daily budget?" → "300" → 300 per day); "let's create Meta ads" starts it straight away when the connector is set up, otherwise AIC says which connector and where. Choices: (1) **Workflows are data** (`config/workflows.json`): how they are recognised (subjects; action words make it direct; words like "report" or "pause" mean something else), the inputs (typed: text, choice, money per day, start date, end, web address), the steps (what the owner does by hand, how the team does it), the connector and the inputs that make the spend. A new workflow is a new entry; there is no code per workflow, and a test checks every entry is complete. (2) **Recognition is plain matching, not a model call**: the runtime adds one line to AIC's prompt per owner message ("the owner may mean …" or "the owner asked for …") and one for the run in progress (what is known, the one thing it waits for). It costs nothing and can't drift; anything vaguer is AIC's judgement through `list_workflows`. A maybe-match starts nothing until the owner's yes; the tool refuses otherwise. (3) **Answers are read by code**, the way the owner says them (an amount with a per-day, week or month word, today/tomorrow/weekdays/dates, "for 2 weeks", "keep running", a domain, an option's words), so the owner's first sentence fills what it can and AIC only passes free text it understood. The run records what was asked; an input with a value is never asked again. (4) **Two modes**: `guide` shows one page per step with the owner's answers filled in (Back / Next on the card, or "done" in the chat), works without the connector and never hands anything to the team; `do` needs the connector and hands the work to the Orchestrator → Marketing with a brief (the answers, the approved spend and how each step is done), through the same ticket path as any job and the same open-jobs limit. (5) **Spending money**: the owner's stated budget is their authorisation for that campaign, and only for it. Before anything is created AIC reads the plan and what it spends (daily budget, total and dates, or "about N a month until you stop it" for no end date; "in your ad account's currency" when none was said) and asks "Shall I create it, Sir?". The yes must be the owner's own message, later than the one the money was shown in (the card's "Yes, create it" sends exactly those words in the chat; no route spends). The brief tells the team to publish at exactly that budget from the start date and never raise it or add funds; this overrides the Meta and Google Ads skills' "create paused" for workflow briefs only. (6) Secrets: none new. Meta and Google Ads sign in through their existing connectors (Pipeboard, AgentCore Identity or a key in Secrets Manager), so tokens stay where every connector's are. (7) One workflow at a time per chat; starting another stops the first. The owner's message was cut off at "If there is a capital inside,"; asked what they meant, not guessed.

127. **Apps are built while the owner is away; taste questions are asked along the way and never waited on (2026-11-09).** The owner: "we should create apps and unless I say explicitly, I'm gonna be away. So create it by yourself… build the entire application until it's finished… Ace should be able to ask me questions throughout the project: do you like this feature? How should it act in this page compared to this page? What's the CTA in this ad?… for ads it's a bit different, for apps also, but the questions are still going to be there." Choices: (1) **App build is a workflow** like the ad ones (`config/workflows.json`, `app-build`): department Apps, no connector, one mode (the team does it), recognised only with an action word ("build me an app…", "make me a game"), never for a change to an existing app ("harder", "fix", an app's own chat) or a complaint about AI City. (2) **Away by default** (`away_by_default`): unless the owner says they are here ("I'm here", "ask me", "let's go through it together"), an input with a default is not asked; the default is used, marked as assumed, and becomes a taste question. The idea comes from the owner's own sentence. With nothing paid, the team starts at once: the owner's direct request is the go-ahead, an exception to "a job starts only after AIC asked" (decision 98) that applies only to this workflow, only on the owner's own message with an action word. When they say they are here, AIC asks one question at a time and "Shall I build it, Sir?" as before (decision 122's rounds), and "you decide" or "I'm off" switches back. Money is never assumed: an ad's daily budget has no default and its spend still needs the owner's yes (decision 126). (3) **Taste questions** are one shared mechanism for every kind of work (`TASTE` / `tq-…` in the ledger): the team's managers and workers ask with `ask_taste` (question, two to five options, the default they are going with) and carry on at once; AIC files one for each assumed input. Each workflow names its own topics (`taste_topics`: for apps, whether a feature stays, how one page behaves next to another, button words and the main call to action, colours and layout; for ads, headline and CTA button, the leading image, keywords to leave out), which reach the team in the brief. (4) **Answers adjust, never block**: the owner answers from the card ("Your say", a tap) or by telling AIC, which raises open ones one at a time when the owner is talking to it (`answer_taste`, the owner's own message only). The same answer as the default changes nothing; a different one goes as the owner's instruction to the agent who asked, or to the Orchestrator for the team when that agent has gone home or the question was AIC's; after the job is finished AIC offers a change (for an app, a new version). Nothing waits: there is no `waiting_on_owner`, no Chief of Staff round, no push. (5) Not built: an iPhone card, and taste questions of a finished run are only raised by AIC (the card shows running runs).

128. **AIC's real-time voice is OpenAI GPT-Live first, Nova Sonic second (2026-11-09).** The owner wanted a more natural voice than AWS offers and chose OpenAI. The most natural one OpenAI offers today is `gpt-live-1` (full duplex: it listens while it speaks, with smooth interruptions; $0.05 per minute, billed to the owner's OpenAI key). It runs in **client delegation** mode, so it stays only the voice: when a request needs the backend, the HUD sends the owner's words to AIC as a normal spoken chat message and returns AIC's answer (`session.commentary.append`), which GPT-Live says in its own words. AIC (Haiku on Bedrock) remains the only brain; the agents stay Anthropic-only. The key never reaches the browser: the HUD posts its WebRTC offer to `POST /voice/live`, the API opens the call with the key from `aic/prod/openai` and returns OpenAI's answer; audio then flows directly between the browser and OpenAI. When GPT-Live can't open (no key, no access, an error), Nova Sonic (decision 120) takes over, then the standard voice. Model and voice are in `config/models.json` (`voice.live`); the prompt is `agents/voice_live.md`. Not tested against OpenAI itself (no key in tests): the call is faked in `web/test/live.test.ts` and `backend/tests/test_voice_live.py`.

129. **Settings → Connectors is organised around the business: each department and the tools it works with (2026-11-09).** The owner first showed the Claude desktop app's Customize page (Connectors/Skills/Plugins, Yours/Discover, search, + Add, a table with logos) as the model, then corrected it the same day: "I feel like we're just building another Claude desktop… I want something that's gonna have more power and more focus on thriving a business". Kept from the model: a table of what is connected with logos, a catalog to browse, search and + Add. Choices: (1) **Departments, not Claude's tabs.** No Connectors | Skills | Plugins tabs: a **Connected | Browse** switch and department chips (All departments, Every department, then the twelve). Connected is one table, Name (logo, what it does) | Type (MCP, Web API, Package, Mail & calendar, Code; a Your own or Built-in badge) | Used by (the departments) | Status (✓ Connected or what's needed); a row opens its settings. Picking a department filters the table to the tools it works with, and adds **What <department> still needs** (its essential tools not yet connected, each connected in one click) and **What <department> knows how to do** (its built-in skills, plus the owner's own skills, which teach every team). Browse shows each department's own section (name, mission, N of M essentials connected, one button for its essentials, its tools with Essential badges, and what it knows how to do). (2) **Connecting a tool gives it to the departments that list it** in `config/departments.json` (shown as Used by and on the card); which agents may call it is still the per-role setting on the row. Per-department access (a Sales-only tool) isn't built: it needs Cedar and policy changes. (3) **Catalog source: `config/departments.json`**, served by the existing `GET /departments/presets`. Its servers each answered an MCP initialize when added (decisions 113, 130); each has a one-line description, most a sign-up link, and the departments that use it. Fetching a public MCP registry live was rejected for the page: its entries are untrusted, unchecked and mostly package servers that can't be added in one click (they need AIC's approval card, decision 81). AIC's `add_connector` searches the official registry when the owner asks for something outside the catalog, and the page says to just ask AIC. (4) **Skills sit with the departments** as "what it knows how to do"; + Add offers Tool by link, Mailbox or calendar, Write a skill (name, one sentence on when to use it, instructions; saved as a SKILL.md through the same `POST /skills` and checks) and Upload a skill. (5) **Logos ship with the HUD** in `web/public/logos/` (`scripts/fetch-logos.mjs`, run by hand): simple-icons (CC0) in the brand's colour where it has the brand, otherwise the site's icon fetched once and kept only if it is a real PNG of at least 32 px and under 64 KB, otherwise a lettered tile (Canva and Freshdesk today). Served from the HUD's origin, so `img-src 'self'` is unchanged and the page makes no outside request. Rejected: hot-linking a favicon service (a CSP change, and it tells a third party what the owner browses) and an icons bucket (infra for 100 small files). (6) Connecting from Browse is the same `POST /connectors` as the department switches; the card then shows the existing sign-in card or secure key field, and the page looks again every 2.5 s while a tool is being set up. (7) Settings → Connectors no longer shows the Save bar: who may read mail saves itself when changed, as before. Settings → Departments (the switches and setup guide) is unchanged. (8) Not built: the same view in the iPhone app (its Settings stays native; noted in PRODUCT.md §6).

130. **AIC offers the service a plain request needs; the owner never has to know the word "connector" (2026-11-09).** The owner: a non-technical user won't say "add the LinkedIn MCP"; they'll say "find a person on LinkedIn", and AIC should suggest the service, guide the sign-in, and then do the work. Choices: (1) **No new tool or card**: `add_connector` gains `why` (what the owner asked for, in a few words) and may be called when the owner's own request needs a service that isn't connected, not only when they say "add". The existing approval card is the one-tap question, so nothing connects without the owner's tap, and the tool is still offered only on turns that are the owner's own message (never from an email, page, report or skill). (2) **The checked catalog first**: a name is looked up in `config/departments.json` (id, label, and new `aka` names such as "jira" or "facebook ads") before the MCP Registry, so common services resolve to a server the city already checked, without a registry round trip; `source` is `catalog`. A Shopify store asks for the store's name first. (3) **Plain words**: the card reads "Connect Indeed so I can look for jobs? You sign in on Indeed's own page; your password never comes to AI City." with the link, where it came from and who may use it after, and its buttons are Connect and Not now. AIC's prompt forbids "MCP", "connector", "server", "OAuth" and "API" unless the owner used the word. (4) **Carry on automatically**: when the card appears, the HUD keeps the owner's latest message (if under 30 minutes old) for that service in this tab's session storage. When the service turns ready it sends "<Service> is connected now. Please carry on with: <their words>" once, as the owner's message. These are the owner's own words captured by the HUD, not text the model wrote, so nothing the model says can be replayed as the owner. Declining or removing forgets it. (5) **LinkedIn can't be connected, and AIC says why**: LinkedIn publishes no server that searches people or reads profiles (its only official one, Hiring Pro, posts a job). Third-party ones drive a logged-in browser as the owner, which LinkedIn bans accounts for (see decision 121). `not_offered` in `config/departments.json` holds that sentence and the catalog services that do the job instead (Apollo.io, Hunter); the tool answers with it and writes nothing. (6) **Business tools the owner named join the catalog** where an official hosted server exists, each checked on 2026-11-09 with an MCP initialize (the same check as every other entry): Indeed (`https://mcp.indeed.com/claude/mcp`, 401 with OAuth metadata; Sales and Research), Freshdesk (`https://<desk>.freshdesk.com/mcp`, 401 with OAuth metadata; Customer Support; asks for the desk's name) and draw.io (`https://mcp.draw.io/mcp`, 200, no account; Application, IT Infrastructure, Marketing, Product, Operations, Media). "AWS" is another name for AWS Knowledge (the city's own AWS account is IT's through Terraform, not a connector). YouTube, TikTok and Instagram have no assistant connection of their own: like LinkedIn they are in `not_offered`, which points to Metricool, Ayrshare and Blotato, the catalog tools that post and report for them. Zendesk (no official hosted server found; Freshdesk, Intercom, Gorgias and Help Scout are in the catalog) and "Evergen" (not identified) aren't added. Not tried against real Indeed or a real model: the tool is tested offline, and the card and resume with Playwright fakes.

131. **The support desk: a customer's message goes to the team that can fix it and back to the person who sent it (2026-11-09).** The owner: "something coming in on Slack from customer support… the customer support agents will create a Jira ticket or whatever tool they're using, which could be free… to the IT department or sales department or whoever is responsible… and then get back to the person who sent the ticket… that the issue is fixed… and that they can reach out again." Choices: (1) **Built on standing orders, not a new kind of agent** (decision 125): a Customer Support standing order is the intake; its goal gains the desk's instructions (`open_ticket` with where to reply: the Slack channel and thread, or the sender and message id). (2) **AI City's own board is always the record** (`SUPPORT` in the ledger), so it works with no paid tool and every step shows in Work; Jira or Linear, when connected, gets the issue too and the key is kept, and the desk tells the teams to keep it current and close it. (3) **The desk moves tickets in code, not by asking a model**: every 5 minutes, on the standing orders' existing schedule (no new infrastructure), a new ticket becomes a job for the named department through the Orchestrator; when that job ends, a reply job for Customer Support; when that ends, the ticket is closed. The tools (`ticket_fixed`, `ticket_replied`) move it at once, and a finished job moves it even when a tool was forgotten, its report becoming the note. Two failed tries leave it Stuck on the board rather than looping. (4) Its jobs carry the order's id, so like rounds they skip the owner's open-jobs limit and the iPhone push. (5) The reply is written for the customer by the team that fixed it (`ticket_fixed`'s one line) and sent by Support in the same thread, never by the fixing team. (6) Not built: a ticket form for customers on the web, priorities and due times, and reopening a closed ticket (a new message in the same thread is recognised as the same ticket, so it is not filed twice; Support answers it in the thread).

132. **Organic content is made and published by Media without asking; paid reach stays with the owner's budget (2026-11-09).** The owner wants Media connected to Canva, Higgsfield, ElevenLabs, "Evergen" and draw.io with skills, making content that "marketing can deploy… or media can deploy to YouTube and TikTok and Instagram if it's just organic content." Choices: (1) **A workflow** (`organic-content`, decision 126's data, no code): brief → plan → make → check → publish, department Media, away by default like the app build (decision 127): "make a TikTok about…" starts the team at once, assumed choices become taste questions. Recognised only with an action word and never with ads words (boost, paid, budget, campaign). (2) Because nothing is spent, the owner's request is the go-ahead to publish; boosting and ads are refused in the brief and stay with the ads workflows and their spend rule. (3) "Evergen" is taken to be **EveryGen AI** (images, video, music, voice from a brief; OAuth MCP at everygen-ai.com, checked live), added to the catalog for Media and Marketing; draw.io's official server was added by the connectors work (decision 130). If the owner meant another tool, it is one catalog entry. (4) **Publishing**: the platforms publish no MCP servers. The team uses the platform's own API when the owner added it (decision 130's flow for web APIs: YouTube Data API, TikTok Content Posting API, Instagram Graph API, with the owner's developer app and sign-in), otherwise the publishing tool that is on (Metricool, Buffer, Ayrshare, Blotato; decision 118). Not built: uploading video files through the web-API bridge (§1.11 has no file uploads), so a YouTube or TikTok upload through the official API needs the platform's pull-from-link option or a publishing tool; Instagram publishes from a link. With no publishing account, the team saves the finished files and captions to Knowledge and says which account to connect.

133. **Sales follows up on stale deals on a schedule, in the CRM or AI City's own board (2026-11-09).** The owner: "following up on sales pipelines". Choices: (1) **A free deal board** in the ledger (`DEALS`) for businesses without a CRM, with plain stages (new lead, contacted, meeting booked, proposal sent, negotiating, won, lost), usable by Sales and by AIC ("add a deal: Acme, 5,000, talked to Bo"). (2) **A Sales standing order** is the schedule ("follow up on my pipeline every day"); its goal gains the follow-up rules: open deals with no contact for 7 days, from the connected CRM (HubSpot, Pipedrive, Salesforce, Attio, Close) or the board; one personal follow-up per deal, sent through the connected mail or CRM, logged on the deal, and recorded as `<deal>#<day>` so nobody is chased twice in a day; won and lost deals never. (3) Sending mail keeps the mail rules and approvals it already has (decided by the Chief of Staff under Autopilot). (4) The board shows in Work → Sales pipeline (open deals by stage, "Needs a follow-up" once a deal is stale, closed ones below). Not built: editing deals from the screen (the owner tells AIC) and syncing the board into a CRM connected later.

129. **Fable 5.1 only for the Orchestrator, Media, Research and Legal (2026-11-09).** The owner: "reduce the amount of Fable… only use Fable for media department and the orchestrator… media, research, legal… No one else should use Fable 5.1. All the other ones should use Opus 5.5." Choices: managers and the Chief of Staff move from Fable to Opus 5.5 with no fallback (Opus has nothing above it to fall back to), workers stay on Opus. `departmentModels` in `config/models.json` names the departments whose manager and workers run on Fable (falling back to Opus once on refusal or no verdict, as before). A role's application inference profile copies the role's own model, so those departments call Fable through its system profile; the runtime's IAM lets managers and workers call the department model too (`dept_models` in `infra/modules/agents/main.tf`). Existing agents' model labels correct themselves on their next turn. AIC and the utility calls stay on Haiku 5.5. **Revised the same day:** the owner then asked for every Department Manager on Fable 5.1 again ("make each department manager for all departments Fable 5.1 agents"). Now: the Orchestrator and every manager on Fable 5.1 (Opus once on refusal or no verdict, each with its own application profile); workers on Opus 5.5 except in Media, Research and Legal, whose workers run on Fable (`departmentModels.roles` is `["worker"]`); the Chief of Staff stays on Opus 5.5. Then: "chief of staff can still be a Fable agent": the Chief of Staff is back on Fable 5.1 (Opus once on a refusal).

134. **Departments on demand, demolition, and a livelier City: cabs and a mailman (2026-11-09).** The owner: "even though I have some fixed amount of a selection of fixed departments, I should still be able to build new departments on demand… the manager should be a Fable 5.1 agent. And unless I explicitly say that the workers on that department should be a Fable 5.1 agent, then they should be Opus 5.5 agents. And I should also be able to destroy the building, and there should be a cool animation for that as well", plus an entrance animation on page load, New York yellow cabs that take workers to work and home, and a mailman on a bicycle carrying the Orchestrator's instructions to each manager and the replies back. Choices: (1) **One path for building and demolishing** (`backend/src/aic/shared/hierarchy/departments.py`), used by AIC's new tools `build_department` and `demolish_department`, by `POST /departments` and `DELETE /departments/{key}` (body `confirm: true`), and so by the City and Settings. (2) **Models**: the manager is hired with the first job on the managers' model (Fable 5.1, as for every manager). Workers run on Opus 5.5; only when the owner explicitly asks for Fable workers does the department record carry `worker_model: "fable-5-1"`, which `department_override` (`shared/models.py`) reads for workers, ahead of `departmentModels`; the host reads the department before a worker's turn, and the factory stamps the model on hire. The worker role's IAM already allows Fable (Media, Research and Legal workers use it, `dept_models` in `infra/modules/agents/main.tf`), so no IAM change. (3) **Names**: only a preset's own name or key is that preset; "Customer Success" builds its own department even though the Orchestrator's aliases send that word to Customer Support. Built departments count against `max_departments` (only the owner's own, beyond the twelve). (4) **Demolition keeps history**: the record stays with `state: demolished` and `demolished_at` (so `ensure_presets` never puts a demolished preset back, and the ledger's tickets and costs keep their department), `list_departments` leaves it out, every live worker goes home (`_walk_home`), every open part of the department is cancelled with a note and handed back to the Orchestrator (one instruction per job: re-plan with another department or report what is left), the manager retires, and one `department.demolished` event goes out. The Orchestrator is told which departments the owner demolished and can neither use nor recreate them; only the owner builds one again (`rebuild_department`, same lot). Standing orders that name a demolished department reach the Orchestrator, which refuses the department and re-plans. (5) **The City** (`web/src/city/scene.js`, pure parts in `city-state.js`): a demolished tower's lot keeps its place until the next snapshot so the other towers don't move; a new tower is built floor by floor behind scaffolding with a tower crane (3.2 s), a demolished one is hit by a wrecking ball and sinks into its dust (2.6 s); on page load every tower rises in turn with a little bounce (once, on the first snapshot with towers). Towers are drawn into their own canvas for this and baked back into the ground when all animations settle (one bake, not one per tower). (6) **Cabs**: two per street, cruising both lanes by the clock (the scenery redraws at 20 fps instead of 8 while they drive); a hired worker walks to the first kerb a cab can reach (never across the lawns between the houses), rides the street and avenue legs in a cab, and walks the last steps in through the doors; going home the same way back. (7) **The mailman**: one rider, parked by HQ. A new goal from the Orchestrator to a manager is a letter handed to him (an envelope hops across), cycled over the streets to the manager at the tower's door, then he rides back; a goal reaching done or failed is the reply, picked up at the tower and handed to the Orchestrator. Letters to the same tower and direction ride as one bundle ("x3"); the queue holds at most eight. The flying parcel between the Orchestrator and a manager is replaced by the mailman; other parcels stay. (8) **Reduced motion or a hidden tab**: no entrance, construction or demolition (towers simply appear or go), no rides (letters count as delivered at once), cabs drawn parked. Test hooks: `_anims`, `_mail`, `data-built`, `data-demolished`, `data-taxi-rides`, `data-mail-out`, `data-mail-back`, `data-cabs`. The iPhone City bundle was rebuilt from the same scene. Not built: per-department tool access, renaming a department, or demolishing from the iPhone app (the City panel inside it is the web one, so it is there wherever the web panel is shown). **Addendum (the same day, the owner's screenshot of streets ending at the green):** no road ends inside the map. Every street spans the town and the 48-tile margin (`MAP_MARGIN`); HQ is a tower's height on the wide map so its street is the first row's; houses go in blocks between the streets rather than under a street; the avenue continues past the houses on a phone. Cab lanes run the full road and a cab's opacity ramps over the last six tiles at each end (`edgeAlpha`), which sit under the map's faded border. Rejected: wrapping cabs at the visible screen edge (they would pop when the owner pans) and streets that bend around the houses (more turns, no gain).

135. **Departments hand work to each other through tickets, mirrored in Jira (2026-11-09).** The owner: Customer Support files a ticket "through Jira… to the IT or to application, whoever is suitable, and that… department manager will assign the ticket to a worker… and then send it back to the customer support manager… And this whole thing should be cohesive… without me giving an input. So there should be tools used to file tickets, from different departments to other departments." Choices: (1) **One board for both**: a customer ticket (decision 131) is a handoff from Customer Support with a customer to answer; a handoff (`kind: handoff`) is the same record from any department to another, moved by the same 5-minute step: to the receiving department through the Orchestrator (its manager assigns a worker), `ticket_fixed` ends the work, the result goes back to the filing department as a new job, and its manager carries on and closes it with `ticket_replied`. No second desk, no second schedule. (2) **Managers only** file (`file_handoff` exists only in a manager's tools); workers ask their manager. Receiving is implicit: the job arrives like any other. (3) **Bounded**: a chain of handoffs is at most four departments (`reply_to` keeps the chain), a department can't file to itself, the same open title isn't filed twice, and one department has at most ten open handoffs with another. A refusal tells the manager to finish with what it has and report what's missing, so nothing waits on the owner. (4) **Jira**: when the Atlassian connector is ready, every ticket and handoff is marked Jira; the team is told to create the issue in its project or component (or use the key the filer passed), assign it, comment progress and move it to Done, and records the key with `ticket_note`. AI City's board is always the record, so the flow is the same without Jira. (5) **The City**: handoff jobs travel Orchestrator → manager and back, so the mailman (decision 134) already carries them as letters; a tower-to-tower route wasn't added. Not tested against Jira itself: the issue is faked in `backend/tests/test_company_handoffs.py`.

136. **The company brief: the owner says once what the business is, and the city runs it (2026-11-09).** The owner: "let's say my business is actually running, for example, Zalando… can I then just tell them to work properly". Choices: (1) The brief is one record (`COMPANY`/`brief`: the owner's words, where customers write, and whether it runs), set by telling AIC (`set_company_brief`) or in Settings → Company (`GET`/`PUT`/`DELETE /company`). (2) Every Orchestrator, manager and worker turn reads it at the end of its system prompt, with its department's job in the business and who it hands work to (a fixed table in `company.py`: Support → Application, IT, Finance, Operations, Sales, Product; Marketing → Sales, Media; Sales → Finance, Legal, Marketing, Support; …). AIC doesn't: it routes. (3) **"Run it" is three standing orders with fixed ids** (decision 125): Customer Support watches the support channels every 15 minutes (the desk), Marketing nurtures leads daily and hands qualified ones to Sales, Sales follows up daily. Turning it off turns them off; turning it on again never duplicates them. (4) Nothing new runs that the owner didn't ask for: other departments work when work reaches them, by handoff or by the owner.

137. **One CRM for Marketing and Sales, and leads move between them by handoff (2026-11-09).** The owner: "we should be able to use, for example, Salesforce for our sales pipeline and marketing pipeline… a feature that they have in common." Choices: (1) Salesforce and Pipedrive join HubSpot in Marketing's tools, so the same connector serves both departments. (2) **The split is Salesforce's own**: Marketing owns Leads and Campaigns, Sales owns Opportunities; in HubSpot the lifecycle stages; on AI City's board a `team` per deal, with two new stages (being nurtured, qualified for Sales). (3) **MQL → SQL is a handoff** (decision 135): Marketing's manager files it to Sales with the lead as `ref`; on the board the deal becomes Sales' and "qualified" the moment it's filed; in Salesforce the team converts the lead. Sales contacts it, `ticket_fixed` says what happens next, and the result goes back to Marketing, which takes the lead out of nurturing. (4) Sales' follow-ups (decision 133) read only Sales' deals; Marketing's daily round reads its own leads. Not tested against Salesforce or HubSpot: both are faked in `backend/tests/test_company_handoffs.py`.

138. **Nothing starts unless the owner said so to AIC (2026-11-09).** The owner: "don't start on anything unless I have explicitly said okay, I have an idea. And then I will talk to Ace about the idea, and Ace will instruct the orchestrator… it's not just projects. It could be anything." Checked everything that runs on a schedule: (1) **Standing orders** (decision 125), and with them the support desk's intake and Sales' and Marketing's rounds (131, 133, 137), exist only when the owner created one: AIC's `add_standing_order` now refuses on any turn that isn't the owner's own message (a report, a reminder, a connector's or another agent's message), and the HUD's route is the owner's own action. (2) **The company brief is off by default** (decision 136): describing the business starts nothing; it runs only when the owner says to run it (`running: true`, from their own message or the switch in Settings), and Settings shows on or off and since when (`running_since`). Turning it off turns its rounds off, and the desk leaves the tickets and handoffs those rounds started where they are until it is on again. (3) Tickets and handoffs move only inside work the owner started: a customer ticket comes from the owner's own standing order, and a handoff is filed by a manager inside a job the owner (or the owner's round) started; nothing reacts to a Slack message, an email or a connector event by itself. (4) The other schedules start no work: the sweep re-sends work that already exists, retire-idle sends idle agents home, reminders notify, the calendar import only reads, the warm ping wakes AIC without a message, and the self-improvement loop stays disabled. (5) The path is always owner → AIC → Orchestrator, for a whole project or one small task; workflows already start only on the owner's own message (decisions 126, 127, 132). Tested in `backend/tests/test_owner_starts_everything.py`.

139. **A jurisdiction-aware knowledge base: every item knows where and when it holds (2026-11-09).** The owner: "if I add… the tax laws in Sweden on Monday, and then on Wednesday I add another tax laws, but for Switzerland… they should all be separated by year. Country… if I have a application that should be deployed in each country… GDPR won't be necessary in the US… but in Europe, especially in EU, GDPR is one important feature." Choices: (1) **Metadata on every item, found on ingest and editable**: jurisdictions (ISO codes, US states, EU/EEA/EFTA, global), year and in-force period, topic, document type, language; Haiku 5.5 reads the start of the text with the file name and folder as hints, the name alone fills the gaps, a failure never fails the item. It runs in the `ingest` step so the first ingestion already carries the attributes and the state machine is unchanged (no infra change). (2) **Stored twice**: on the item record (the truth) and as Bedrock metadata (`.metadata.json` + inline), with a `countries` list beside `jurisdictions` so "US" also finds California's items without a prefix filter. (3) **One search per market**, filtered to the market, its parent regions (Sweden → EU, EEA) and global, answered in separate `<market>` sections; the record re-checks every hit, and when the filter finds nothing or is refused a wider unfiltered search is post-filtered, so one country's law never lands in another's section even if OpenSearch can't filter the new fields. Rejected: one combined search with all markets (mixes rules, the thing the owner warned about). (4) **Newer years supersede, older stay**: same topic, a shared jurisdiction, a later year in force; the older item is marked, not hidden or deleted, and agents use it only when the task names that period. A coarse topic means a 2026 Swedish tax law marks every older Swedish tax item; marking (not dropping) keeps that safe. (5) The region map is a config file (`config/jurisdictions.json`), the prompt rule two sentences in the manager and worker prompts. Not verified against the real KB: Bedrock's `listContains` on the new attributes in the OpenSearch Serverless index (docs/KNOWLEDGE.md §15).

140. **Executive Leadership: a CEO, COO, CFO and CTO who advise and never run work (2026-11-09).** The owner pasted a long "AI City — Executive Leadership System" brief and said: "Take all of these instructions with a grain of salt, because I already have said a few instructions before… those come before this. So make sure to implement this but don't make it take over anything. Just add it as an extra bonus on top of everything else so that we have more robustness and we can scale our business wherever we want to, or if there's financial decisions." Choices: (1) **Advisory and subordinate to the owner's earlier rules.** The chain stays owner → AIC → Orchestrator → managers → workers (decision 138: nothing starts unless the owner said so). Executives govern direction and judge outcomes; they never assign, hire, start, execute, go around a manager, override the Orchestrator or decide for the owner. Where the brief conflicts with an earlier rule (for example "the CEO is the founder's strategic counterpart" versus "the owner decides"), the earlier rule wins. (2) **Stateless calls, not new runtimes**: one Fable 5.1 call per executive (Opus 5.5 once on a refusal), role `executive` with its own application inference profile so their cost shows on the bill, made from AIC's and the Orchestrator's runtimes (which may now invoke that profile) and from a small `executives` Lambda for reviews. No AgentCore runtime, queue or IAM to start work. (3) **Tools are read-only and run by code**: the executive's read tools (status, jobs, costs, handoffs, deals, department reports, the company brief, the knowledge base) are called before the model, each through an allowlist and the policy gate, audited; the model then writes one JSON recommendation (recommendation, reasons, risks, facts with source, assumptions, confidence), which `send_recommendation` files (`EXECREC`, 62 days). Rejected: giving them a model-driven tool loop, which costs more and adds nothing a fixed read set doesn't give. The allowlist denies anything else whatever a prompt says. (4) **When they act**: the owner asks AIC (`ask_executive`, only on the owner's own message); the Orchestrator consults them on a big or cross-department decision (`ask_executives(question, who)`; its prompt says only then, only the executives who add value, no meetings for their own sake); optional reviews (CFO cost review, COO bottleneck review) are off by default, switched on only by the owner in Settings → Executives, and produce a short line in AIC's chat, never work. Routing without names is by subject; a new initiative goes to all four; nothing recognised goes to the CEO. (5) **Advice stays advice**: it comes back as the tool's answer; the Orchestrator and managers decide. Under Autopilot the Chief of Staff gets the last week's advice in its context and may weigh it; the owner's notes and rules come first. (6) The CFO reads AI City's own cost estimates and, when the owner added them, the business's financial records in the knowledge base; it never commits money or sets a cap. The CTO advises IT Infrastructure, Application and Data & AI through the Orchestrator and never implements. (7) The owner can switch each executive off (`PUT /executives`); an executive switched off is not asked. (8) The City: HQ's top-left window shows the boardroom (four small figures at a table), static, no new animation. Not built: an iPhone screen (the web page works there), the knowledge base in scheduled reviews (they read the city's own records), and reviews for the CEO and CTO. (9) **Permanent, at home until called in** (the owner, later the same day: "they could go home… the orchestrator can actually instruct them to get to the office, and they actually work in the HQ department"): four agent records (`exec-<id>`, kind `executive`) that always exist and are never released or retired; `dormant` at home (no calls, no cost), `active` at HQ only for the length of a consultation (`executive.office` both ways, home again even when the call fails). Their memory is their own advice: the last five recommendations of that executive go into its prompt. The City gives each a house and the existing commute (cab or walk) to HQ's plaza by the boardroom window. They don't count in "Agents working now" or against a department's cap. Tested only with fake models (`backend/tests/test_executives.py`, `web/test/executives.test.tsx`, `tests/infra.test.ts`) and the mocked backend in Playwright (`web/e2e/commute.spec.ts`); not yet seen on AWS with Fable.

141. **Any tool connects end to end: checked sign-up links, it turns live by itself when the owner comes back, and a green dot says so (2026-11-09).** The owner: if a service needs an account first, the link must work and take them to the page; after creating the account they come back to AI City (Settings or the chat) and see it connected, with a green dot that shows the connection is live. Choices: (1) **Sign-up links are checked, live and offline.** `scripts/check-signup-links.mjs` fetches every catalog sign-up link (2xx; a 401/403/429 bot wall counts, since it proves the page exists; Facebook answers 400 to anything but a browser). The bot-walled ones were opened in a real browser on 2026-11-09. The first run found four dead links (DocuSign, Fireflies, Meta Ads' 400, Metricool), now fixed. Higgsfield, Ramp, Captions, Pika, Blotato and vidIQ now go to their actual sign-up pages instead of a homepage, and Indeed got one. Unit tests (vitest and pytest) hold the offline rules: https, no secrets in the query, every service that needs an account has a link. Some links are still a homepage or app root where the service has no separate sign-up page (Granola, Vanta, Gong and others), and GitHub's sign-up page answers 403 to automated browsers (it is GitHub's own address). (2) **Every sign-in and key card says "No <service> account yet? Create one on <service>'s site"**, in the chat and in Settings, opening in a new tab without opener or referrer, with "then come back here: this updates by itself". (3) **It turns live by itself when the owner comes back.** When the tab gets focus or becomes visible again, the HUD asks `GET /connectors` (and `GET /mail/accounts`) only for what a card or a waiting request still follows, and feeds the answer through the same event path as the live socket, so a sleeping tab or a dropped socket doesn't leave it pending. Settings → Connectors reloads its table the same way. No new route. (4) **AIC says it, once**: when a service the owner was connecting turns ready, the HUD adds "<Service> is connected, Sir." to the chat, written by the HUD (not the model, so it costs nothing and can't be wrong) and then carries on with the waiting request (decision 130). (5) **The green dot**: a small green dot with a gentle 2.2 s pulse marks every live connection (the table's status, the catalog card, the done card in the chat, a connected mailbox). It stands still under `prefers-reduced-motion`. (6) **Errors in full**: a failed connection shows its whole reason on its card in the chat ("Acme didn't connect: …"), in the table's status ("Problem: …") and in its details. API errors keep their fix ("… Try again in a moment."). (7) **Every catalog server has a connect path, checked by probing each one's OAuth metadata on 2026-11-09**: 67 sign in with one tap (OAuth with dynamic client registration), 18 need no sign-in, and 2 ask a name first (Shopify store, Freshdesk). 12 have OAuth without self-registration (Artlist, Asana, Box, Crunchbase, DocuSign, ElevenLabs, GitHub, HubSpot, PagerDuty, Slack, Unsplash, Xero): they are marked `setup: own_app`, their catalog card says it takes a few extra steps, and their card takes the owner's own app's client id and secret, or an access token instead (the existing bearer-token path the GitHub "I have a token instead" uses). Whether a pasted token is accepted is up to each service. 2 publish no OAuth metadata (Jira & Confluence, Pipedrive), and the connectors Lambda decides on its probe at add time. Agents never sign up, sign in or pay: the owner does it in their own browser. Not tested against the real services: the journey (sign-up link → new tab → come back → live) runs in Playwright with the service's page faked.

142. **One design system: the look and motion of shared pieces live in tokens, and the brand is an amber "AI" pill with "City" (2026-11-09).** The owner asked for a logo in the Add skill button's colour and type, no glow around the buttons, and everything common in the UI (buttons, effects, animations) centralised so that changing one button changes all of its kind. The accent fill, ink, edge and shadow, the button radius, weight and press, and the motion curves and durations are CSS custom properties on `:root`; `.btn-primary`, the send and voice buttons, toggles, segmented switches, feature icons, today in the calendar and the wordmark read them. `--accent-shadow` is `none`. A unit test guards against copies of the gradient, glow or curve creeping back. The City scene keeps its parchment pixel style (decision 107 and the approved direction), so it is outside the tokens. The iPhone app is unchanged; its wordmark is a separate follow-up if the owner wants it there too.

143. **AIC can read and change anything in AI City for the owner (2026-11-09).** The owner asked for no questions and no restrictions: anything on a screen, AIC does when asked. AIC runs the HUD's own routes as the owner through a direct invoke of the API Lambda (`aic_call`, AIC's role only), so every check the HUD has still applies and nothing new can be done that the owner couldn't do on a screen. Three lines stay because they are the city's invariants, not limits on the owner: no secret ever passes through the chat (AIC opens the page instead), only the owner's own message can trigger it (so a document, email or worker report can never steer it), and nothing reaches IAM, the policy file or Terraform (the API has no such route). Spending limits became something AIC may change on request, since they are the owner's setting; the self-improvement loop still may not.

144. **The iPhone's voice is a light behind the chat, not a row above the box (2026-11-09).** The owner circled the eye, the flat voice line, "Tap the mic to talk" and the Speak replies pill on the iPhone and asked for the eye to go completely and the sound bar to sit behind the text, integrated, so more of the chat shows. The row is gone. The voice is drawn as the web's aurora behind the chat text and the composer's glass: blue while the owner talks (the microphone's level), amber while AIC talks, a low glow while AIC thinks about a spoken message, and nothing at all when nobody talks, so a typed conversation has no decoration. AIC's level follows word boundaries, because the iPhone's system voice does not report loudness; that is the honest signal available without replacing the voice. "Completely" is taken to include the small eye in AIC's status row, which is now a small amber light; the drawing stays only in the app icon. Speak replies goes too, as on the web (decision 112): the choice produced silent or unwanted replies, and one rule is easier to trust: spoken in, spoken out; typed in, text out. The iPhone still has push-to-talk (tap, talk, tap to send), not the web's continuous conversation, and no hands-free mode (decision 69). The glass theme now reads the accent and motion tokens of decision 142, which makes the amber buttons a shade lighter at the top, like the web's.

145. **No top bar on the iPhone's AIC tab (2026-11-09).** The owner circled the glass bar with the "AI CITY" title and asked for it to go, title included, "so we can read more text". The bar carried nothing the owner needs while talking: the tab bar already says where they are. So the AIC tab hides its navigation bar and the conversation runs to the status bar, under a soft blurred fade that keeps the clock readable. The two buttons that lived in the bar's corners (the "While you were away" clock and Clear conversation) float there as small glass circles, since both are still wanted and neither deserves a row. Decision 108's "the wordmark is the AIC tab's title" no longer holds on the iPhone; the wordmark stays where the app introduces itself (sign-in, starting, failure). The other tabs keep their bars: their titles name lists and forms, and their content should not hide under one.

146. **On the iPhone, AIC's theme change shows on the City tab only, and unknown pages are ignored (2026-11-09).** Decision 143 lets AIC change the theme and the open page on the owner's screens. The iPhone app applies the same `hud.action` event, with two choices of its own. The native screens are dark glass in every appearance (decision 103), so a "light" theme has only one place to show: the City tab, which until now followed the phone's own appearance and now follows the owner's choice when there is one. Drawing a light version of every native screen would be a redesign nobody asked for. And `open` maps only the pages the iPhone has a screen for (AIC, City, Tickets, Costs, Open items, Settings); Apps, Knowledge and Skills exist on the web only, so those events do nothing here instead of landing somewhere misleading. As on the web, only the two known actions with known values are accepted; everything else in the event is ignored.

147. **The logo is a skyline tile and "AI City" (2026-11-09).** The owner liked the direction of the amber "AI" pill, not the pill. An app-icon-like amber tile with three towers and a spire, then the name in the heavy rounded face, reads as a brand at 28 px and in both themes; the tile reads the accent tokens (decision 142).

148. **Tap rings and a soft click everywhere, one switch to turn them off (2026-11-09).** The owner asked for a sound and two signal-like circles on every click in the browser and the iPhone app. The click is synthesized with Web Audio (no file to load, very quiet); the rings sit in a layer that never takes a pointer; reduced motion drops the rings; Settings → General turns both off per device.

149. **One dropdown for the whole HUD (2026-11-09).** The owner asked that every dropdown follow our theme in a style of our own, shared across the app. `Select` takes the same props and `<option>` children as `<select>`, so every screen switched by changing one tag; the list opens in a portal (no card clips it and no wrapping label re-opens it) and is a proper combobox + listbox for keyboards and screen readers.

150. **On the iPhone a tap is a finger that lifts where it landed, and the click is a system sound (2026-11-09).** Decision 148 puts rings and a click on every click in the browser, at pointer-down. A phone is touched far more than a page is clicked: most touches are scrolls. So the iPhone answers when a finger lifts within 12 pt of where it landed, and stays quiet for scrolls and drags; a signal on every scroll would be noise in the ear and in the hand. The touch observer sits on the window and never recognises a gesture, which is what keeps it from delaying or stealing any touch (the City map and the switches included). The click is the browser's tone written to a 60 ms file and played through the system-sound service, not an audio session: that is the one path that obeys the silent switch, plays at the ringer's volume, never pauses the owner's music and cannot disturb the app's own voice session. A light haptic rides along because a phone can. The switch lives in a new General section of the iPhone's Settings, which had none, and is kept on the device like the browser's.

151. **Files is its own store next to Knowledge, found by Titan vectors kept on each record (2026-11-09).** The owner asked for one place in S3 for photos, videos, PDFs and any file, which the departments read and write, and for VAIC to find any file from a vague memory ("the sunrise thumbnail with the Porsche") and show it in the chat to view and download. Files is a separate store, not a view onto Knowledge: Knowledge is what every agent reads as reference, so each item is chunked into the Bedrock Knowledge Base and can surface in any answer, and only VAIC may add to it on the owner's word; holiday photos and draft thumbnails don't belong in every agent's search, and departments must be able to write here. It has its own private, KMS-encrypted, versioned bucket (`aic-prod-files`) and table, and reuses the knowledge base's code (presigned single and multipart uploads, routing by format, Haiku vision and scanned-page reading, MediaConvert with the same role, Transcribe). Each file gets a description when it lands (an image caption, a document's text, a video's frames and transcript, plus its name, folder, tags, who added it and the dates in words) embedded with Titan Text Embeddings V2, the model the owner already allowed, now also under `embeddings.files` at 512 dimensions; the vector stays on the record as float16 (1 KB) and search is one pass in the Lambda or runtime: cosine plus a keyword bonus, with dates VAIC works out from "last spring". A second OpenSearch index on the knowledge collection was the alternative: it would be faster past tens of thousands of files but needs index creation through the deploy role, a new data-access rule and signed HTTP from three places, none of which can be checked offline; at the owner's scale the pass takes well under a second and costs nothing idle, and the move to an index is listed in `docs/FILES.md` §11. The pipeline is event-driven (S3, MediaConvert and Transcribe state changes on EventBridge) instead of a second Step Functions machine, because each file needs at most three steps and every step's state is on the record. Departments write the way workspaces work: through the session-tagged workspace role, only into `hires/<their id>/files/`, and the files Lambda adopts each drop and marks it with the department read from the ledger, never from what the agent wrote; managers may now assume that role too (tag `mgr-…`), only for this. VAIC shows files with `show_files`: the chat keeps only the file's id and words, and each card asks for fresh 15-minute addresses when drawn, so an old chat never holds a link that has expired or leaked. The iPhone app gets the chat's cards (preview and Download through the share sheet); its own Files screen and upload are a follow-up.

152. **AIC becomes VAIC, said "Vice" (2026-11-09).** The owner rebranded the assistant to VAIC (Vice Artificial Intelligence City), pronounced like Vice City, and wants AIC gone from the app. Everything the owner reads or hears changed; identifiers and AWS resource names did not, since renaming them would recreate live resources for no visible gain. "Vice" is an ordinary English word, so it is turned into VAIC by the HUD's speech cleanup with exceptions ("vice president", "vice versa", "Vice City"), not by Transcribe's vocabulary, which cannot make exceptions. The old name still wakes VAIC, so habit never fails the owner.

153. **The City's boxes are refractive glass (2026-11-09).** The owner asked for the City's info boxes to be see-through and "glassy like the skill we have" (refractive-glass-ui). They bend the town behind them with one SVG filter defined in `CityView`, fall back to blur where `backdrop-filter: url()` is unsupported, and keep a parchment tint strong enough for the ink text. This replaces the wood-framed parchment of decision 107 for those boxes only; the map, its buildings and the zoom tiles keep the pixel style.

154. **Fable 5.2 replaces Fable 5.1 everywhere (2026-11-09).** The owner asked for every Fable agent to move to Fable 5.2, released the same day and on Amazon Bedrock (`anthropic.claude-fable-5-2`, checked in Anthropic's model docs). It costs less than 5.1 ($8 / $40 against $10 / $50). Records written before the switch keep working through a legacy-name map rather than a ledger migration. The Bedrock prefixes stay global and us as for 5.1; if 5.2 is refused in this account, the Opus 5.5 fallback answers instead of the agent failing, and the error is logged.

155. **The owner's files travel with a build as references; workers get images as images and documents as text (2026-11-09).** The owner asked that VAIC use a stored file when asking for an app: "follow the best practices mentioned in that file or just create the UI with inspiration of an image". VAIC finds the files with the same search and passes them to `delegate_to_orchestrator` as `references` (a file id, `follow` or `inspiration`, a short note); an unknown id starts nothing. They are stored on the task like the brief and found by every part below it, so the Orchestrator, the managers and the workers each see the list with one rule: a `follow` file is the owner's guidance, an `inspiration` file shows the look, and the contents of any file are data, so instructions written inside it never override the owner, the policy or the agent's role. The worker's assignment turn carries the material itself rather than a pointer: up to three images go to the model as image blocks (converted from Converse blocks to the Agent SDK's message blocks), and documents as their text inside `<file-content>`, read through the hire's own session-tagged role. Doing it at the turn, not by asking the worker to fetch, means the look and the rules are in front of the model from its first token and the read uses the same scoped access as everything else a department reads. A file that can't be read is named with the whole reason. The project chat (and the main chat) shows the references under "References for this build" with their previews.

156. **Files' previews come through a private CloudFront distribution with signed URLs; the key pair is made inside Secrets Manager (2026-11-09).** The owner asked that VAIC make a short-lived link when they ask for a file, a separate one to download it, and that "the images should be visual thanks to CloudFront showing the image from that S3 bucket". Previews and thumbnails (the chat's cards, the Files grid, the PDF frame, the video player) are now CloudFront signed URLs (canned policy, 15 minutes, one object) on a distribution in front of the still private bucket: Origin Access Control, HTTPS only, a trusted key group, the bucket policy letting only that distribution read `files/*` and `thumbs/*`, and the KMS key letting CloudFront decrypt for the account's distributions (naming the one distribution would make the security module depend on Files). The signature is not part of the cache key, so a thumbnail leaves S3 once and is served from the edge to every later view; the grid only ever loads the 480 px thumbnails. Downloads stay S3 presigned with `attachment` and the file's current name, because S3 sets the "Save as" name from the request and CloudFront would need the query forwarded to the origin (breaking the cache) to do the same. Every view and every download gets its own link, issued by the api when the owner's screen asks; VAIC never sees one (its `city_api` reads have them removed, its tool returns card ids), and no link is logged or stored. The private key never meets Terraform: Terraform creates an empty secret `aic/prod/files-signing-key`, invokes a small Lambda that fills it with a new RSA pair only when it is empty and answers the public key alone, and registers that public key and the key group; only the api role may read the secret. This was chosen over a documented one-time step for the owner (who is often away from the Mac and can't run MFA-bound commands from the phone, see the 2026-11-07 notes) and over Terraform's own key resource (which keeps the private key in state). To rotate, empty the secret and apply. Making this work needed one structural change: the Lambda bundle's zip no longer depends on the functions' settings (every function runs the one "app" bundle; a test guards it), which had made the api's env and the HUD's CSP depend on Files in a cycle.

157. **VAIC's message box follows ChatGPT's layout (2026-11-09).** The owner wanted the words to fill the width before wrapping and a plain mic. Every control now sits inside one rounded field as a borderless icon, so the text column is as wide as it can be; when the words need a second line the box switches to two rows (words on top, buttons below) and stays there until it is emptied, so it never flickers between layouts while typing. The dictate button uses the plain microphone glyph; the voice button keeps the waveform.

158. **The logo is "VAIC" with the AI set apart (2026-11-09).** After the rebrand (decision 152) the owner wanted the logo to say VAIC with the AI special. Of four drafts (amber AI, an AI chip, a leaning amber AI with a spark, an underlined AI) the leaning amber AI with a spark reads best at 22 px in both themes and keeps the word whole; the skyline tile stays as the mark.

159. **The logo is a striped sunset over a skyline, and VAIC is drawn, not typed (2026-11-09).** The owner asked for a complete redesign of decision 158's tile and leaning AI. The new mark is a round sunset cut by dark stripes with a skyline in front: it says "City" and nods to the Vice City look behind VAIC's name, and still reads at 24 px. The word is drawn as SVG strokes so the web and the iPhone show exactly the same letters regardless of fonts; the A without a crossbar mirrors the V, and the amber "ΛI" with a spark over the I keeps the AI special, as the owner asked before. The mark's sunset colours are fixed (a logo, not a theme colour); the "AI" amber comes from tokens with a deeper pair on the light theme. The iPhone app drew the same geometry as SwiftUI shapes, and its app icon replaced the eye icon that decision 144 had left behind.

160. **In the voice conversation, echo is judged by when a phrase was said, not by how much it resembles VAIC's words (2026-11-09).** The owner could not get through to VAIC and was not heard when talking over it. The earlier echo guard compared everything heard with VAIC's last reply, even after VAIC had finished, so natural answers that repeat the question's words were thrown away; and a loudness gate looked at the seconds before Transcribe's final instead of the phrase itself. The rule now has one place (`web/src/voice/heard.ts`): a phrase is the owner's unless it started while VAIC was audible (its reply or a thinking cue, with a 600 ms tail); then it needs two words VAIC did not say, or one interrupt word, or, if VAIC was just cut off by the owner's voice, one word of the owner's own. Live partials stop VAIC only when they are mostly new words, so a misheard echo does not silence it. A leading run of three or more of VAIC's words in order is cut from what is sent. A reply whose finished text continues the one the owner cut into is not read aloud; it is identified by text because `ace.reply` carries no turn id, and when there is too little streamed text to tell, the reply is spoken rather than risk dropping an answer. The barge-in detector reads the level of the microphone Transcribe already uses rather than opening a second one (which on iPhones can mute the first and makes the browser re-tune echo cancellation). Considered and not done: counting replies to skip stale ones (wrong if the backend merges or cancels turns) and closing the mic while VAIC speaks (the owner asked to be heard while it talks).

161. **The logo is a Vice-style neon sign (2026-11-10).** The owner found decision 159's logo too busy, did not like a flat amber tile with a V, and asked for GTA Vice City's style. The logo is now our own 80s Miami neon drawing, not the game's lettering or artwork: a sunset with stripes and a palm, and VAIC as leaning neon tubes, pink V and C around a cyan "ΛI" so the AI still stands apart. It leaves the HUD's amber accent for buttons and states (the logo is the brand, the accent is the interface). The glow is an SVG blur on the logo only; decision 142's no-glow rule is about buttons.

162. **The logo goes back to decision 158 (2026-11-10).** Having seen the striped sunset (159), a flat tile with a V and the neon sign (161), the owner chose the amber skyline tile with VAIC and the leaning amber AI. 159 and 161 are withdrawn; 158 stands, and the favicon is the same amber tile. The iPhone app follows: its Wordmark is decision 158's again and its app icon is the favicon's tile as a square.

163. **Connectors that fail say why in full, and one mail request is one card (2026-11-10).** The owner asked VAIC to connect Canva: nothing happened in the chat, and Settings said only "AgentCore Identity refused the sign-in setup (AccessDeniedException)". Asking for Outlook put three app-password cards in the chat at once ("Microsoft", "Outlook", "Outlook MCP"). (1) Every connector error now carries AWS's error code and whole message (`aws_error` in `lambdas/connectors.py`; the bus event keeps up to 1,000 characters instead of 300), so an access denial names the action and resource that were refused; AWS messages name actions and ARNs, never request bodies or secrets. (2) The connectors Lambda is the first runtime role to create an OAuth provider (the mail providers are made by Terraform with the deploy role), and creating one also checks, and on first use creates, the account's default token vault. AWS's own BedrockAgentCoreFullAccess policy allows that through `bedrock-agentcore:*`; ours named only the provider actions. The connectors and api Lambdas now get `bedrock-agentcore:GetTokenVault` and `CreateTokenVault` on `token-vault/default` only. This is the likely cause, not a proven one: no AWS call could be made from the session that wrote it, and if Canva still fails the error now says exactly what is missing. (3) After the owner taps Connect on a connector card, a failure while setting it up comes back to the chat as a problem card with the full reason, instead of disappearing with the approval card. (4) Microsoft has turned off app passwords (basic authentication) for Outlook.com and Microsoft 365 mail, so the app-password fallback for Microsoft is removed (backend preset and the HUD's Outlook.com preset): Outlook connects only through Microsoft sign-in (AgentCore Identity's Microsoft provider, `mail_providers.microsoft`, docs/RUNBOOK.md). Until the owner registers the Microsoft app, VAIC says so plainly and adds nothing, and `POST /mail/accounts` answers 400 with the fix. Gmail keeps its app-password fallback. (5) Asking for the same mailbox again, in the same turn or later, reuses the card already waiting (same provider, still signing in) instead of adding another account; two different IMAP mailboxes are still two. VAIC's prompt says to call `add_connector` once per mailbox, not once per name of the same service.

164. **A connect card stays where it appeared, and the conversation carries on below it (2026-11-10).** The owner, in their words: while a card with fields is waiting, they should be able to keep writing and get answers "even under the box with textfields". The HUD drew every connect card after the whole conversation, so it stayed pinned to the bottom and later messages and replies went above it. Now each card remembers the chat line it appeared after and sits just before the owner's next message (VAIC's reply in that turn stays above it); a key or sign-in card that follows an approval card takes its place, and a card that changes (key → done, error) keeps it. The message box was never disabled by a card, and the backend never waited on one: `add_connector` returns at once and no turn checks pending connectors. The card stays usable after later turns.

165. **Whole-code review: what was fixed and how each case now behaves (2026-11-10).** The owner asked for a review of every line for bugs and for steps that break in combination, and to fix what was found. Thirteen read-only passes covered the backend, HUD, iPhone app, Terraform and workflows; the confirmed bugs were fixed with tests. Behaviour that changed: (1) An approval the owner does not answer before the Step Functions wait ends is not a refusal: its card is withdrawn (`approval.withdrawn`) and the agent is told the request closed unanswered; an `app.publish` card is left open, so a late yes still publishes. The approvals Lambda may now put events on the bus. (2) A worker's dispatch lease lasts as long as that role's turn timeout, so a long worker turn is not started twice; a slot is released once, and a turn that returns early still marks its work finished. (3) `deploy.yml` ignores only `docs/**`, the top folder's `*.md` and `ios/**`; prompts and skills are code and deploy. (4) The permissions boundary allows the SNS endpoint actions and `cognito-idp:AdminCreateUser` the roles already ask for, and the scheduler may `dynamodb:BatchWriteItem`. (5) A message the API refuses with a 4xx is shown as not sent and never re-sent from the outbox (HUD and iPhone); a failed button action shows its error as a notice. (6) The Chief of Staff makes no model call after three failed tries on an item: it goes to the owner, or to Autopilot's fallback. (7) `publish_draft` and a workflow hand-off each take a short claim, so a double click or a retry runs them once. (8) Smaller: the voice turn keeps its session id, the microphone is released when the screen closes, a CalDAV password is sent only to the host the account names, a broken recurrence rule or one bad support ticket or standing order no longer stops the rest, a truncated tool input does not crash the turn, mail problems inside an app read as "unavailable", a bad mail port or request body answers 400, the PyPI connector starts with the right arguments, the iPhone ignores app-chat replies in VAIC's chat, and the Costs meter and attachment chips keep their size. Left for the owner or for evidence from production, listed in the review report: what a city reset deletes, the dispatcher's 10 second read timeout, and the medium and low findings that change behaviour the owner chose or that could not be reproduced.

166. **The City opens first, a less regular town, and a walk into a building (2026-11-10).** The owner asked for a less symmetric city with real-world proportions, to click a building and stand in front of it, pick one of its projects, fly up into that project's floor, click an agent who turns to face them, a fast way out, and the City as the landing page. Decided: (1) `/` and the return from sign-in open the City (an unknown route falls back to it); the VAIC logo still opens VAIC. (2) The town keeps its rules (streets edge to edge, houses never on a road, a demolished tower leaves its lot and nothing moves) but HQ and the plaza sit in the middle with the houses under them and the towers alternate east and west of it; each tower's footprint (8 to 11 tiles) and height (11 to 15) come from a hash of its name, not its headcount, so decision 117 holds (a tower never grows) and the map never reshuffles as workers come and go; rows are as tall as their tallest tower, so streets fall at uneven spacing, and between two towers there is a side street or now and then a strip of park. Three silhouettes keep the skyline varied. (3) The visit is plain React with CSS 3D transforms over the map, not WebGL: no new dependency and no bundle growth beyond the component, and it matches the pixel style. (4) A project on the visit is an open job at the top of the department's ticket tree; its floor holds the department's manager and every worker on a part of it (by ticket or `ticket_id`); oldest project lowest, from floor 2. Apps are not shown there (they are finished work, under Projects). (5) Every step is a hash route (`#/city/visit/<dept>[/<ticket>]`), so Back undoes it; Esc goes one step out (after closing an agent's card), **City map** goes all the way out in one click. Reduced motion cuts instead of flying and turning. (6) Clicking HQ still opens its card. The iPhone app's embedded City is regenerated with the new town; the visit itself is HUD-only for now.

167. **Names read at any zoom; zoom is smooth and continuous (2026-11-10).** The owner, zoomed out on the City: "I can't even read the department's name", and wanted to zoom out and in to any scale, more smoothly. The map is pixel art drawn at one art pixel per screen pixel when zoomed out, so a tower's painted sign (5 px letters) was unreadable. Now every tower and HQ gets a name plate in real 12px screen text over its sign while the zoom is below 2.6 (above that the painted sign reads on its own and the plates step aside); a long name wraps instead of being cut. Zoom is no longer seven fixed steps (1 to 6): it is continuous from the whole countryside on screen (border included) to 8×, eased in the frame loop around the pointer. A mouse wheel, trackpad scroll or trackpad pinch zooms in proportion to how far it moves, a two-finger pinch follows the fingers, the + and − buttons and keys ease by 1.5×, double-clicking a tower glides it to the middle, Fit fits the whole town exactly, and reduced motion jumps instead of easing. Below 1× the map is smoothed rather than pixelated so it does not shimmer. The plates are aria-hidden: the Departments list already names every building. The iPhone app's embedded City is rebuilt with it.

169. **The logo is the owner's eye, and VAIC is set plainly (2026-11-10).** The owner circled the browser tab icon (the amber tile with the skyline), the logo tile beside the name and the "VAIC" with the leaning amber "AI" and spark, rejected all three, and gave their own drawing of an eye (`docs/brand-input/eye-owner.png`, kept with the circled screenshot `current-marked.png` as the brief). The eye is traced with potrace into filled outlines (`web/public/eye.svg`) so it is the owner's drawing, not a redraw; it has no tile, circle or background. The tab icon (`favicon.svg`) is the same outlines on a square canvas coloured by `prefers-color-scheme` inside the SVG: dark ink on a light browser, near-white on a dark one; no PNG favicon is added because every current browser takes the SVG and a PNG cannot follow the theme. `apple-touch-icon.png` (180 px) is the white eye on black, the drawing as the owner made it, since iOS fills transparent home-screen icons with black. In the HUD the eye is a CSS mask filled with the theme's text colour, so it follows the HUD's own light/dark setting, not the browser's. The word is "VAIC" in a clean sans (Avenir Next where present), semibold, 0.3em letter spacing, in the same text colour; it replaces decision 158's amber italic AI and spark (the `--wm-ai` tokens are gone). The iPhone app's `Wordmark` does the same with an `Eye` template image from the same SVG, and its app icon (`ios/scripts/make_icon.swift`, now drawing `web/public/eye.svg`) is the white eye on black. The VAIC screen's eye (`ace-eye.png`, decision 107) is the same drawing and is unchanged.

170. **The City's names are the painted signs again; the logo opens the City and the VAIC tab is Chat (2026-11-10).** The owner did not like decision 167's name plates (12px screen text in dark boxes over every tower) and asked for the names as they were before, so the plates are removed and each tower's painted sign is its name again; decision 167's smooth, continuous zoom stays. The owner also asked to rename the "VAIC" tab to "Chat" and to remove the City tab, making the logo the way to the City: the logo (decision 169) is now a link to `#/city` ("VAIC — the City", marked current while the City is open) instead of a button to VAIC's screen, the top bar's tabs are Chat, Work, Projects, Files, Knowledge, Skills, Costs and Settings, and VAIC's screen keeps its address `#/ace`. The iPhone app's tabs are unchanged.

171. **A building is an isometric cutaway, never first person (2026-11-10).** The owner saw the street view and the first-person office of decision 166 and asked for something else: no first-person view anywhere; the building's floors seen from above at an angle like an isometric office illustration, with the floor of the picked project clear and everything else barely visible, the whole building included; a better-looking office in pixel art (windows onto the skyline, desks, monitors, shelves, plants); workers that look like real people, still in pixels; and smooth, physical motion between the map, the building and a floor, with no frame that just switches. Decided: (1) This replaces points 3 and 5 of decision 166 where they describe the street, the fly-up and the eye-level office; the routes, what a project is, who sits on a floor, the exits and the landing page stand. (2) Every floor of a building is the same room, sized for its largest team (up to 12 desks on a wide screen, 8 on a phone); a floor is one canvas of pixel art drawn in code (`web/src/lib/officeArt.ts`, original art, not a copy of the reference pictures) with its people as real buttons over it, so keyboard and screen readers work as before. (3) The tower is drawn whole: a lobby, a floor per project, two storeys above. The lobby and upper storeys are a shell that is always at 7%.
